WOMEN'S CHAT.

Two of the London hospitals will greatly benefit by the Coronation, as they are favourably situated for letting seats te sighteeers.—At the Westminster Hospital facing the entrance to the Abbey, a stand will be erected to accommodate 2,000 people, and as some of the seats will let for £2O or £25, the hospital coffers ought to overflow. !St George's Hospital is also favourablylsituated at Hyde Park corner for viewing the procession on bath days,and this institution expects to make £7,000 outlof the sale of its seats. The members of the staff will be given one seat each for one day, and for the rest from three to twenty guineas will be charged according to position. There is, as yet, very little evidence of a great demand for seats, but they will probably readily let at the hospitals, as those who pay high prices for the seats will have the satisfaction of knowing that their contributions go to a good cause, instead of into the hands of greedy epeaulators. M. Santos-Dumont has announced his intention of snaking an aerial flight round the dome of St Paul's, on Coronation Day. The famous aeronaut hagarrived with his alrship, and should nothing interfere with his plans. the performance will rival the attractioos of the Corona- tion itself. London has not yet seen anything so interesting as a navigable airship, though now that M. Santos-Dumont proposes to conduct his experiments in this country,we may hope to see something of it. There is some talk of a flight from London to Birmingham,but it is more particularly in London that the people would turn out in their millions to give M. Santos-Dumont a welcome. The King and Queen have finally given up Marl- borough Houee,and will reside in futur,e at Buckingham Palace when in London. Marlborough House, which was formerly the residence of Princess Charlotte and Queen Adelaide, was settled upon the King on his corn- ing of age, and it is not without many regrets that the Queen has left what has been the home of her married life. It wet on the 6th of March 1863, that the fair young princess made her triumphal entry into London, and during the forty years that have nearly elapsed, Her Majesty became muoki attached to Marlborough House. The mansion was full of her most cherished personal effects, and beloegia-s, most of which have now been removed to Buckingham Palace. The gardens at Buckingham Palace go far to redeem the blunders and shortcemings of its architects, for nothing could ever make the building beautiful in itself. Moreover, they are of an extent of which few who have never enjoyed the privilege of visiting them are likely to form an accurate ides. Altogether the richly diversified grouods entend to over forty acres, and contain many noble trees, relics of the old Arlington gardens, when there was not a house between Piccadilly and the village of Kensington. The lake covers several acres, and is also very picturesque with its swans and waterfowl. Th. crnamental water has been redraincd, whilst the ravilions and summer-houses have been re-decorated, end the art of the gardener has done much to improve the handsome pleasure grcunde. Riches seldom make people genercue, and it is often paid that the poor give more freely than the wealthy. An illustration of this was recently afforded at the open- ing of an exhibition of pictures in the east end of London at which Lord Crewe was present. These exhibitions, which are intended for the elevation of the pocrer clause, are supported by voluntary gifts, mainly by the a entire of the hardworking people who come to see the pictures. There is however usually a ceremonial open- ing and en this particular occasion, some three or four hundred people, chiefly from the wealthy districts of London, were invited to meet Lord Crewe. Pfore they left. Canon Barrett made a special appeal for the assist- sleet of the well-to-doe in such useful works, but the total amount given,so it is said,was only thirty shillings, er less than one penny each. The London Flower Girl's Mission affords, en the ether hand, an excellent exa nip:a of what the poor may do for thenasalves. The Mission was started to help the poor girls who sold flamers and watercress in tho streets, a calling which seemed:to include an unusually large proportien of cripples. For these, however, the Mission started artificial flower-making, which hoe now developed ioto an organised industry, affording an excel- lent means of livelihood to a large number of girls, who would otherwise lie at a great disadvantage for a means of earning a Haim The exhibition of artificial flowers, which the Duke of Argyll recently opened at Glasgow, was the work of these girl., and it is hoped to found a similar institution in that city. A new type of artificial flowers has appeared, and is in'high fanour. These flowers which usually take the form of roses—but more closely resemble rosettes—are composed of panne and cirCpe, or chiffon and silk, touched with paint, embroidery or jewellery. One and all have a dew drop in the beart. For trimming gowna, and evening wraps, these flowers ly) are in great request, they even appear in the hair, and on dainty evening shoes. Considering the truly exquisite imita- tions of Nature which are now offered us, it is a little curious that we should turn aside from these, and, in their place, take up this aceeventional form of trimml/4ag, which from an artistic point of view leaves much to bs deSined. Our old frieud the mailer hat has appewerl again, but under a somewhat new guise, vi., the brim turned up all round. 'lbis particular form oeheadgear boasts little in the way rf oinamentation, a silk scarf drawn round the Crown and arranged in a moderate siz'd bow in front being the meat desirable horn the leading milliners' standpoint. The sailor hat as we have long known it, with straight brim and plain ribbon band, will not be worn by women who drees well. It is the hat of the multitude, a stare of affairs net acceptable to smart wcmen. 'lna same will soon have to be said of the full-pouched Russian coate. Almost every other woman one meets, when taking one'a walks abroad, is wearing a garment of Ws descr pt,cn. and the day is fast approaching when the fashionable modiste will tutu from this too- greatly appreciated model to another less well-known The time and labour expended in building these coatees must be enormous, judging by the quantity and diver- sity of the ' rimmings that adeen the.m. and the effect nine times c.. 0 of tea is neither artistic or refined. • plain neat toilet nowadays is positively restitil but there is letle hope that the great majority of women will indulge in anything of the kind this side of the Coronation. Either plain or spotted muslin curtains are preferred this spring both for reception and bedrooms. These are of course. The home-worker however, must remember that, her fr;ll* must not be too full—a ens- tomary, fault—ow too wide. The frills should fell grane- fnlly, and lightly, when the curtain is dtaped. For Casement curtsies taselara silk is still being used, but the edging in the case ot the best id of lace, tassels be- ing somewhat too port xer. A work we shall de well to thoughtiullv and carefully prose is Miss Lilian Whiting's " The World Beautiful in Cooks " (Sampson, Low, Marsten and Co., 59). In these days when cheap trashy novels a.e being poured Into the marker, such a work as Miss Whiting has given us Is invaluable, for et leads our thoughts back to great "valiters and thitikers whom, alas, are an far leiw remembered Una they r bould he, It will moreover fill t with a desiie to sead,and read carefully, their works, which percha ace we have hithe:so felt little or no in- clination to take ug.

ILLUSTRATED FASHIONS.

- Each succeeding week now brings With some novelty in the world of dress, and there is more of interest consequently to chronicle in the annals of dress and fashion. The shop windows are full of fascinating goods of every imaginable kind, and the colours this season are, if possible, more alluring and delicately beautiful than ever. Everything in dress tends towards a harmonious blending of shades, and though several tones may be employed in the construction of one toilette, they are all in ac- cord. Violent contrasts are considered democi, by the followers of Madame la Mode at present, and the dress, hat, gloves, and even shoes in some instances are all in unison. One cannot help being struck with the fact of how much is done now-a-days to assist the home worker in the construction of her toilettes. Skirts of the latest cut, tucked, braided or embroidered can be obtained at prices ranging from £1 to any number of guineas, with or without material for the bodice. Thete require only to be lined and set into a waist band. Lengths of silk of different colours and qualities are also sold ready tucked and veined all complete for making up into smart shirt blouses. These are invaluable aids to the amateur worker, who invariably finds great difficulty in keeping the tucks even and at regular distances from each other. One is

SMART BLOUSE. -SO apt to go astray in cutting a material which has to be tucked, as it is no easy matter to calculate the exact quantity which must be allowed. The smart blouse depicted is made in the latest style, and while 'showing tome tiny tucks is also trimmed with bands of ecru lace, the same lace forming the semi-transparent neckband and cuffs. The material employed is of a delicate green tint. A pretty and useful, material for blouses is taffetas, which has stripes of embroidered net or lace inserted at intervals of two to three inches. Then hats or toques nowadays are made of such fanciful materials that they require little trimming, and what they do have can be bought, whether it be ribbon or flowers, ready to be sewn on.

Ribbons still play an important part tin the scheme of dress, and the patterns and colourings of the newest ribbons form an endless variety. One very pretty kind is patterned with silk woven roses and foliage on a sort of lace back- ground, while many are rendered exceedingly rich looking by a gold or silver thread running through the whole of the weaSingl

- , .For evening wear both thewfirst. and second Empire modes seem to take [precedence, and are generally becoming. The fpretty tea gown Illustrated is of soft greyish blue crêpe de chime, lined with pale pink silk, the softly falling draperies being caught up at one side of the figure above the waist. The collar land yoke are of p.ale pink satin encrusted with medallions of finelace, the loose sleeves, which come only a short distance below the elbow, [having an edging in the form of a band of the same lace appliquéd satin. folds across the figure is much in vogue, and some of the smartest opera cloalo for debutantes this season are distinguished by this feature. But, indeed, the styles ,for evening as for day wear are innumerable.

The newest sleeves, if so they can be called, consist for the most part of merely a shoulder strap of velvet or flowers with a bouffant drapery of tulle or chiffon falling from the shoulder strap, leaving the top of the arm bare. Another charming variety has a series of points of lace which meet at intervals down the outer part of the arm, the points being held together by small jewelled buckles and tiny knots of black velvet ribbon. One of the most chic modes for a black dinner gown consists of a sort of trellis work of sequined or beaded trimming sewed on to narrow black velvet ribbon, the arm showing through the interstices. This Mode was em. ployed with happy 'effect on a white, richly brocaded gown, the sleeves being made of pearl embroidery. The somewhat hard effect was softened by a slight drapery of tulle. Its charms are set forth in my sketch. Point d'eBprit is a delightful material, and much in request for

For young girls soft, thin materials of delicate texture such as erg-pe de claine or mousseline de mie are considered correct. One of the most notable characteristics of ;the novelties in dress fabrics is their lightness and suppleness. Of these, perhaps, silk voile is the most admirable, as it hangs in soft folds and can be obteined in charming designs either shot with different colours or checked. The linens too, which are offered for our wear later on are also a great improvement on the old varieties, arid are silky in texture and very glossy and handsome looking. THE EXAMINER FRID Y APRIL 4 1902

LALL RIGHTS RESERVED.] THE BARON'S CHOICE,.

ALICE ISAACSON (Author of "After Ten rears," 4'e.)

Cusrraa IX. 1 There is beauty in each season as it circles round the year, but perhaps the loveliest of all is early spring, when the earth awakes from her long win- ter's sleep, and clothes herself anew in robes of emerald, jewelled over with the sweet wild flowers that spring to life at every step. Up in the depths of the sombre pine-forests it is Etill gloomy and dark, but beyond their shade the larches are waving their tasselled fringes of tender green, the waxen spikes of the horse-chestnut blooms seem placed in readi- ness for a fairy illumination, the feathery catkins of the hazel-trees have shed their golden dust, and all the shrubs and bushes are bursting into perfect leaf; while from between the lichen- covered stones, and beneath the hedge-rows and along the shady banks, tiny ferns are showing their freala green arrowy fronds, young brackens uncoil their crisp brown rings, sweet violets shed their perfume around., yel- low cowslips an daffodils, wild anemones and the delicate wood-sorrel with its mystical trefoil leaves are carpeting the ground, and soft cushions of variegated moss of. the most exquisite shades of pink and red and green invite the weary passer-by to rest and dream the hours away. Down in the plain the vines have thrust out their curling tendrils an cling around the stems from which still dangle some of last year's withered leaves ' • all is stir and move- ment and life, the life of youth, of beauty and of love' the season of gladness and of hope, when all that is sad and dreary seems to fade from the memory and there is only a joyous looking-forward to brighter and happier days to come. On this glorious May morning, beneath the cherry- trees with their wealth of pure white blossoms we shall once more find Elsa Lorentz. She has just come out from the old verandah-shaded parsonage, and slowly sauntering along the garden paths' into the orchard beyond, is standing now, her golden hair uncovered, her blue eyes raised to where the sweet May sunshine filters through the green leaves -overhead. With her fair lovely face and graceful figure she is no unfitting emblem of the Goddess of Spring herself, as she gazes heavenwards, and for- getful of all around, dreamily watches the flight of a lark that is soaring higher and higher until it seems a mere speck in the wide ocean of blue, and the music of its song almost dies away; then there is a pause and a downward rush, and the bird flings out its wild sweet notes in fuller, richer cadence, until it sinks at last into its nest in the grassy meadow beyond the garden wall. More than three months have passed since that fatal day when the death-angel spread the shadow of his sable wings above Schloss Medemburg ; the spirits of the Brunnen-See have kept their secret well, and those who recall the stately Minna Von Tiircke as she swept from their midst in all the pride of her regal beauty may retain that fair and fascin- ating image undimmed by any ghastly after-thought of horror, for her body has never been recovered, and the mystery surrounding her tragic death must remain unsolved until the last great day when hidden things shall be brought to light, and the all-wise purposes of sin and sorrow and suffering shall be made plain. On the morrow of that awful tragedy Pastor Lorentz visited the Schloss. The fair-weather guests:to whom death and its surroundings were so uncongenial had hastily taken their departure, and after the gaiety and excitement of the past few weeks an ominous silence reigned. By the Baroness his kindly min- istrations and sympathetic words were welcomed, but the unhappy mother, in the utter abandonment of a hopeless grief, refused to be comforted. The interview was a painful one, but the pastor had an-. other duty to perform before returning home, and as he sought the young baron, with the rebellious lamentations of the miserable woman he had just left still ringing in his ears, there was a stern look on his face, the expression that those of his flock whose misdeeds called for reproof had learnt to dread. Conrad was in his own private sanctum, half study, half smoking-room. He had returned early that morning from the hunting-lodge, and weary and depressed, was restingin his arm-chair by the fire: but as the old man entered he sprang up to greet him with hands outstretched and a flush rose in his face, pale from the night's sad vigil. "My kind old friend, I might have been sure you would seek us in our trouble," he exclaimed, "does my mother know-that-you, .?e hem?" Then, as the pastor did not immediately reply, struck with sudden apprehension, he added eagerly—" You are not come to bring us more ill-tidings—tell me of Elsa, she has not suffered—she is well ?" Pastor Lorentz moved towards the fireplace but, declining the arm-chair that the Baron hastily drew forward, he remained standing, his hands clasped upon the top of his stout walking stick, his eyes bent gravely upon the young man's face. "I have already visited the Baroness, and have striven, as yet in vain, to comfort the stricken mother. For Elsa," his voice faltered a little,— "thank God she is well, though sorely' troubled in spirit, but,"—he paused for a moment, then with a change of tone, continued: "Baron von Medem, though it may seem inoppor- tune, there is one question I would fain ask you for the sake of my child's peace of mind—what closer bond was there between you and that unhappy girl other than the mere tie of relationship ?" Conrad looked up in surprise. "Between Minna and me do you mean ?—she was dear to me as a , sis- ter, nothing more,—it is strange, Herr Pastor, that you should ask me such a question now, for although this is no time to speak of love and marriage, I meant to have sought you out to-day, and told you how your little Elsa has won my heart, and that my whole life's happiness rests with her." As though he had not heard the remainder of the sentence the pastor repeated the words " has wonray heart it may be but a young man's fancy! —"Conrad" he continued, with a sudden change of tone, unconficiously using the more familiar name as in the old days when they were tutor and pupil, and laying his right hand on the young man's shoulder, Conrad, Elsa is the only child •of my`old age, the cherished treasure of my life, nay dearer to rim than life itself, and yet God knows I would rather lay her in her grave than that for love of her any man should be tempted to a dishonourable act. Until vester-eve no shadow of care had ever touched her that I could avert, no sorrow had dimmed her happiness, and pure and innocent as a new-born babe she has grown up in our quiet home knowing naught of passion or of strife; but now "—here the pastor's keen eyes rested searchingly on Conrad's face, and the low even tones of his voice were vibrant with emotion—" now I have left her writhing beneath the memory of cruel scathing words that may have been -prompted by causeless jealousy, or may have .been wrung from a wounded heart by a sense of bitter wrong. Your cousin accused her of having stolen your love away from her, the one thing in life she cared for, and vowed that she would rather see my Elsa dead than that she should live to be your wife: it was while speaking thus that, blinded by anger and heedless of da-rer around, she was hurried on to her fate. You alone can tell what ground there was for so wild an accusation, and whatever the future may bring, it behoves me for Elsa's consolation to learn the truth." Conrad for a moment stood silent, speechless with horror, yet half incredulous ;—Minna jealous of his love for Elsa; Minna, who was on the eve of betrothal to another, yet loving him with such reckless self-abandonment that she could utter those mad false words! Impossible—the very thought was profanation of the memory of the dead —and yet even as he rejected it, there rose the remembrance of cutting words and unkind looks, and half-veiled innuendoes' and though he recoiled as from some stunning blow,lhe knew instinctively that the pastor's story was true, and almost felt that by his very blindness and unconscious egotism he had become his cousin's murderer. Oh, if he had spoken boldly as became a man, sonic way out of the tangle might surely have been found, and Minna's wild unbridled passion have been turned into a purer, nobler channel. Some reflex of these thoughts served to deepen the light in his blue eyes that yet looked un- flinchingly into the pastor's face, and though his voice trembled slightly there was no shade of hesi- tation as he replied to the appealing look that met his own. "You have known me, Pastor Lorentz, from boy- hood, and you know that my lips have never, thank God, been stained by the utterance of a he, so you will believe me that there has not been one word of love spoken between my unhappy cousin and myself. Minna was about to be betrothed to a nobleman of great wealth and high position, and though I did not suppose she was deeply attached to him, I fancied her contented in her choice. As for my love for Elsa, it has grown so gradually, so imperceptibly that I cannot tell you when it first began, only I have never loved another woman, and whether she cares for me or no, at least none other will be my wife. But," he added, "I am still bewildered : can you tell me exactly what has passed between your daughter and yourself ?" Thus urged the pastor described the scene upon the ice, Minna Von Tiircke's passionate upbraiclings I and Elsa's wounded pride, adding nothing to the I tak,3 _but_aeeking rather tasters= the_miserahle_girl

frfiose jealous natiits andrife--long serf-indulgence had brought such bitter retribution. As he ended Consad Ton Medem turned away; leaning ha lei/ arm „ against the high carved oaken mantel-piece, he laid his face upon it, at the same tune • stretching out his right hand to his companion; the pastor took it in his own, and for some moments the two men stood in silence thus, their hands clasped in that strong firm grip that means so much of sym- pathy, and help, and friendly consolation and that with men takes the place of a woman's clinging embraces, her kisses and her tears. It was the old pastor who spoke first, and his words rang with stras ge. solemnity through the quiet room. 1 'Lord, what are we that we should judge another —forgive us our trespasses as we forgive them that trespass against us—Amen !" There was a breathless pause; then Conrad raised his head, and it was no shameto his manhood that tears glittered in his clear blue eyes. "Poor Minna, would I could have saved her—but this 'sad story'must lie between ourselves. I would not for the world increase her mother's grief. And. now you will advise the Herr Pastor what I ought to do. Can I not see Elea-to day ?—watild to heaven had spoken out before." "Nay my boy, this is surely, as ythryourself said, no time to speak of love. , There is 'much to be thought of first; what the world will say to 'such a, roarriage"—Conrad made an impatient gesture of dissent, but the old man continued calmly without heeding the interruption,—"and what are 'your gracious mother's wishes; but apart from these considerations it is impossible—Elsa is in no mood to listen to you now; the child is mourning in bitter self-reproach over her last words to the unhappy Minna, words that were wrung from her against her will may be, but that now she would give all in her power to recall. • I dare not say that I should not be proud and happy to, give my treasure into your keeping, but it othmot, must not, be until the shadow ef this great sorrow shall have passed away."

CHAPTE'R X.

And so the conversation ended, as did Other con- versations later with no difforent result; Conrad would have sought an interview with Elsa after the lapse of a few days, but she steadfastly re- fused to see him. From that terrible moment when in the gathering dusk her feet had stood as it were upon the very brink of the valley of the shadow of death, a change had come over the young girl. Into her innocent light-hearted youth there flashed as never before a realisation of the mystery of sin, and as must always be with pure, unselfish souls, she suffered in double measure not only for the wrong done in thought against herself, but for the happy doer of that wrong; and to all Conrad's pleading messages she returned but one answer. ‘f, Father, tellhim to forget me, indeed it cannot be ; surely Minna would stand between us always if I dared to listen to his love."

Possibly the Baron might not so easily have been repulsed had not other claims just then pressed heavily upon his time and thoughts; Frau von Tiircke's uncontrolled grief caused those around to fear lest her reason should give way, and when change of scene and. climate was advised as a last recourse, the Baroness, drawn from her long sec- lusion by anxiety for her sister's health, announced her intention of accompanying her to Italy for the rest of the winter. As soon as the necessary arrange- ments could be. carried out, Schloss Medemburg was left to the care of servants under the watchful eyes of Fraulein Agnes, and Conrad von Medem travelled southwards with the two ladies and saw them settled in that sunny Florentine villa on the banks of the Arno where Minna and her mother had been accus- tomed to pass the winter months. It was his inten- tion afterwards to make a prolonged tour, but to Pastor Lorentz, who came to bid the travellers "God speed," he gave his farewell words for Elsa. "You will guard my darling well for me," he said, wringing the old man's hand," and tell her our parting is only for a time; when the spring comes back and the gloom of the winter is gone, I too shall return, and then, in the sweet promise of future joy, we will forget the sorrows of aat", past." • * * • • •

r.

Until the song of the lark died away, Elsa still kept her listening attitude with a wistful far-off ex- pression in her bine eyes; a passing breeze stirred and rustled the green leaves above her, and shook the white petals of the cherry-blossoms till they fell in a fragrant shower around and were caught in the meshes of her golden hair, and rested like drifting snow-flakes upon her dress. So absorbed was she in her thoughts that she did not hear the quick but noiseless approach of footsteps upon the short grass until they had almost reached her side, and then she turned with a sudden start to meet Conrad von "Acteclem'a eager, ardent gaze. As he saw the radiant- irrepressible joy that illumined her lovely face, dyeing the soft checks with a rosy flush, and deep- ening the curves of the tender mouth, there was no need to utter the question he had come to ask: her hands outstretched in, their glad welcome were im- prisoned in his owr., her slight figure drawn unre- sisting to his heart, and while her fair head drooped against his shoulder he whispered inlow impassioned tones:

" MyElsa, mine at last—ah, sweetheart, you can- not know how I have hungered for the sight of your dear face." For those first few moments of strange bewilder- ing rapture time and sense stood still: the very air seemed quivering in voiceless 'sympathy, the wild birds sang soft love-songs of their own. The May sun poured its radiant beams through the green can- opy above their heads in shimmering light and shade, and the white blossoms drifted around them like the filmy cloud of a bridal veil. But at length Conrad led the young girl to a ins- tic bench encircling one of the old trees near at hand, and seating himself by her side, said with a mischievous gleam in his eyes as they looked into her own.

___ ......... . "Have you nOthing to say to me, my little Elsa, after all these weary weeks of waiting? Do you know you have not told me yet if you love me." She raised her long lashes with a shy swift glance, but just then, as if some slumbering chord had been touched by, his words, a transient shadow crossed the sunshine of her face, and her sweet lips quivered painfully. "I do love you, Conrad, dearly "—she whispered, "but oh, are you quite sure that thisis right? I can- not forget poor Minna's words." The Baron's light tone changed. "My darling," he said earnestly, " it is morbidly sensitive to dwell upon the past; I had not meant to speak of this just now, but it is well perhaps to lay the spectre that has risen between us once and for all at rest. As you know, there was not the shadow of a truth in Minna's frenzied words, the evil she brought upon herself arose from her self-centred ima,,• a.tion ; and Believe me, my dearest, passion such a ers is as different as light from darkness to the ye, pure, deep and true, that draws our hearts toge her. Ws may mourn over her tragic death, though not as those without hope, while a merciful All-Father cares for his sinful creatures ; but for you and me life stretches out before us full of the glorious prom- ise of perfect happiness, in a mutual confidence and

Drawing her gently to him as he spoke, he bent his lips to hers, and in that long, tender kiss the last of Elsa's doubts and misgivings vanished out of sigli,t for ever. Again there was silence between them which von Modem broke at last. - -

"Come," he said, rising, "my mother will be weary of waiting to welcome her new daughter." Elsa looked tip started, "What, is the Baroness here ?" • " Yes, I left her with the good Frau Pastor while I came to seek you. You see;" he added with a smile " I could not tell what my welcome was to be. Three months ago you were so certain you could never love me, and—' a little hand laid lightly on his lips stopped further speech, and when it was promptly captured and held fast, he continued: "What, sweetheart, must I not find faurt then with mine own? So be it, let us talk of other things; you have not asked me yet about any of our friends. Fritz von Derendorf and Countess Falkenstein are to be married in the autumn, and—" "And Frau von Tiircke "interrupted Elsa eagerly. "and poor Count Hopstadt, what of them ?' "My aunt is slowly regaining health and spirits, though she will never be quite what she was be ; as for the Count he will never marry, but he is happier far in his shattered dream than he could ever have been in its realization, for it would have been a bitter awakening to find he was deceived." Slowly sauntering along the shady paths while talking thus, they had at length reached the corner of the old garden-wall overlooking the high road. Conrad stopped and turned to Elsa "Do you remember, dear," he said, "that bright June evening nearly a year ago' when I passed you standing just here with your two girl-friends? So earnest were you, so absorbed, I marvelled what the topic of your conversation was: you might have been three fair conspirators planning some secret treason against your fellow .men." Elsa looked up archly in his face, a dancing light shone in her blue eyes, a blush rose upon her cheeks as she answered with a musical ripple of laughter in her voice: "Would you really care to hear what we were discussing? I remember it allso well. It was no state secret ortreasonable plot, but just when you rode by, we had been wondering, at least Gederele an Frieda had, who was the favoured maid= wli& would be Baron von Ildedein's choice."

lellowing the excellent example of Glasgow, Wolverhampton is holding an Art and Industrial Exhibition this year, to be opened on May Day by H.ll. H. the Duke of.: Connaught who will be accompanied by the Duchess. It can hardly be said, however, that the 'Capital of the Black Country" borrowed the idea of an exhibition from the Scottish city, for long before the gates of the Belvingrove Show were opened,, Wolverhampton had decided upon a similar exposition, but the success which attended the northern enterprise has greatly encouraged the promoters, and helped materially to maintain the keen interest which the people of the Midlands hay all along manifested in the scheme. There seems little doubt but that their enthusiasm and enterprise will be adequately rewarded. Certainly they have every reason to feel well satisfied with the result of their efforts up to the present, for the available space for exhibits has been applied for twice over, and, as thg will be the most attractive and important exhibition held in England since the Manchester one thd necessary patronage of the public confidently may be relied upon when the time tomes for them to be admitted to the grounds. The main buildings, which have been designed by the architects Who planned the structures , which were, so much admired in Glasgow last year, are being erected in the West End, Park, and will occupy 127,215 square feet. They comprise an In- dustrial Hall, a Machinery Hall and a Cana- dian Pavilion, and in their design, precedent has practically, been Ignored, and an endeakour made to reflect artistically their contents and use. The three principal structures—and very hand- some and graceful they will be when comPleted— form a crescent facing the main aveuue and commanding the full extent of the beautiful Park. There will also be other buildings, such as restaurants, a concert hall, etc., covering an additional area of 20,980 square feet. • About thirty-two acres of ground have been enclosed for the purposes- of the Exhibition, and the total capital expenditure will be over £100,000,, the buildings alone costing half that sum. The entertainment of visitors, quite, apart from the exhibits, will be varied and ample, comprising high-class concerts, good music, a water chute, a spiral toboggan, an electric theatre, and a thousand and one - minor attractions. During the summer months some important athletic contests will be held in the grounds, and the inevitable Ping-Bong ,tburnament has not been overlooked.. The Exhibition • will be ready on the opening day—that seems certain—and then the inhabitants of the proud old town will be quite willing to leave the rest to the British public.

On every hand there is the greatest enthusiasm. Throughout the United Kingdom, public meetings are being held to discuss all sorts of schemes for observing and commemorating the great occasion in a fitting manner, and in almost every case the various public bodies are taking a spirited lead and working with might and main to win suc- cess. In London, the Earl Marshal, the Lord Chamberlain, the Lord Mayor, and hundreds of other high officials and willing assistants are strenuously directing their energies to com- pleting the arrangements for the, many great functions of that memorable week, includ- ing the colossal task ot dining half-a-million • of London's poor at the expense of his Majesty. DRAUGHTS.

WARRINGTON LEAGUE. CAIRO-STREET V. WARRINGTON D.C.

The last and most interesting matck of the season was played at Cairo-street, the visitors being the Warrington D.C. Much interest was centred in this match owing to the fact that the destination of the Chalmers Cup depended on the result. The Warrington Club held an advantage, as altheugh their first match with Cairo- street had resulted in a tie of seven games each, they had won every other match, whereas the Cairo-street team had unfortunately to play one man short against the much-improved Latchford team, and they paid the penalty by losing the match by ODC game. It was only necessary for tne Warrington D.O to make the result of the match a tie to enable them to carry off the trophy for the second season in succession, and this they did after a fine struegle. The last game .to be played was between C Powell (Warrington) and W Massey (Cairo-street), the wins at this stage being equate At the ending of the game W Massey seemed a certeire winner, but when victory was within his grasp he made a slip, and C Powell took advantage of this by making atbrilliant " shot," which left the game and also the match a tie, the result being four wins each, and sir draws. THE km CHURCHES,'

THE BRADFORD MEETINGS.

The Bradford meetings of the National Free Church Council were undoubtedly very suc- cessful. The number of representatives was larger by 200 than the number at any previous Council, and the attendance both of the repre- mentatives and of the general public, from the beginning to the end of the session, was very good. Dr. Townsend greatly distinguished him- self as President. His address from the chair was really a great deliverance, and he showed himself to be possessed of the highest business qualities. He kept a close watch on the pro- ceedings, and adhered admirably to the time- table. The incidental little speeches he had to make were in the best form and grace.

===== 0002601_19020404_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PRESIDENT-ELECT.

The nomination of Dr. Parker as President for 1903-1904 was ratified by the Council with enthusiasm. The famous minister of the City Temple is looking forward to his year of office with hope and pleasure. His acceptance of the nomination is a gratifying indication of his return to better health. The meetings next year—at Brighton and with Dr. Parker as President—should be among the most memor- able in the history of the movement. That magnificent pile of buildings, the Dome and the Pavilion, has been placed at the disposal of the Council free of cost, and the Corn Exchange will also be available on the same terms. The genial General Secretary (Rev. Thomas Law) confesses that his only fear is the splendid four-mile promenade, the magnificient piers, and the glorious sea trips.

THE FREE CHURCH YEAR-BOOR.

This volume, which contains a full account of the proceedings at Bradford, together with much valuable information, indispensable to every Free Churchman, is to be published early in April at 2s. 6d. net. The Year-Book grows in value year by year. Last year we believe the sales exceeded by fully one-third those of any previous year, and the National Council's Publication Department is preparing for a still larger sale this year.

===== 0002601_19020404_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
GIPSY SMITH'S AUTOBIOGRAPHY.

This volume has gone into a fifth edition (com- pleting ten thousand). No one is more surprised at the success of the work then Mr. Smith himself. By the way the gipsy delivered himself of a very strong attack on the system of pew-rents at the Bradford meetings. Mr. Smith de- clared passionately that the churches had slandered the man in the street unmerci- fully, and that the great obstacle to the work of the churches among the poor was the pew- rent system. He asked, amid some laughter, if a company of tramps marched into church and took possession of the rented pews, would the congregation rise and sing, "Praise God from whom all blessings flow ?" Mr. Smith was accompanied to Bradford by his wife, younger son, and daughter. The son is a Wesleyan local preacher, and a student at Owen's College,. Manchester. Next year he hopes to enter one of the Wesleyan Theological colleges as a student for the ministry.

REV. H. ELVET LEWIS. The Christian Commonwealth has published a very interesting character sketch of Rev. H. Rivet Lewis," of Canonbury, London, the well- known Welsh poet preacher. The writer says

that he felt while listening to Mr. Lewis's quiet, thoughtful, fragrant sentences that a fit pre- liminary would be half-an-hour's prayer- meeting and a natural sequel consecration

bour. Mr. Lewis has done a good deal of work both in poetry and prose. His life of the great Herber Evans is, perhaps, his most notable recent production.

ILLNESS OF THE REV. THOMAS spunonoN. Great sympathy has been expressed with Ihe Rev. Thomas Spurgeon, his wife, and daughter, in the illness from which they have been suffering. Mr. Spurgeon is greatly beloved for his own and for his father's sake. He has held together the great congregation at the Tabernacle in a manner truly marvellous. Mr. Spurgeon possesses two great :qualities, which) in addition of course to spiritual force, are in- dispensable to the minister of a large church— pluck and business aptitude. A more timid, less capable man would have been crushed under the burden of rebuilding the Tabernacle. Mr. Spurgeon's daughter, Vera, who has been suffer- ing from influenza, is the very image of her grandfather, the famous preacher. MISS MARIANNE FARNINGHAM. The distinguished poet and authoress is restored to health and has been address- ing Sunday-school workers at Wrexham. She said that the teachers ought to be ex- cused from doing other church work, so as to he able to devote their:whole time to prepare for

their classes. Teachers ought to be -willing to tell their scholars all that they had themselves been taught. The work of the !,Sunday-school teacher was a gain to the teacher himself as well sis to the scholar.

THE LONDON WESLEYAN MISSION.

The anniversary was marked by a brilliant sermon by lan Maclaren (Dr. John Watson of Liverpool), and by the absence of the two best- known missioners, the Rev. Peter Thompson and the Rev. H. Price Hughes, M.A. To illustrate the width of the mission Rev. W. D. Walters, the general secretary, told the story of a titled lady who, dying in hospital, asked for a Sister of the People to visit her, and referred to the fact that every month at Clerkenwell 600 bomi-fide working-men remain to the administration of the Lord's Supper. Mr. Walters recently received a letter in Erencla from the Attache of a Russian Embassy, saying that he was investigating the condition of the masses in large cities, and asking for full infor- mation of the Mission's mode of reaching and helping the degraded and poverty-stricken people of London. Dr. Parker the other day referred in warm praise to the work of Mr, Price Hughes in the West-end. THE FREE CHURCHMAN'S LEGAL HANnsocnc, This is tho latest publication of the National Free Church Council. It has been prepared by Mr. Edmund C. Rawlings, a solicitor of the Supreme Court, acting on behalf of the Legal Committee. Mr. Rawlings is a well-known Primitive Methodist, and we believe the legal adviser of his Connexion. He was helped in the work by Mr. J. Bamford Slack, and others. The volume, published at 2s. 6d! net, will answer many questions which Free

ministers have frequently to consider, besides being of great use, also, to all laymen who take an interest in public affairs.

ST JAMES; CHURCH, LATCHFORD.

- - There web a good attendance at the vestry meetings in connection with this church which was held la the schools on Tuesday evening. Toe Vicar (Rev F E Powell) presided.

===== 0002601_19020404_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
SPEECH BY THE VICAR.

At the outset the Vicar delivered a speech on the work of the past year. He said there was nothing very start- ling to present, hut he was thankful to say that during the year they had lengthened their cords and atrengthened their stakes. 'They had lengthened their cords inasmuch as the new mission of St. Hilda had been built, fitted and furnished, and well nigh paid for, and stood on their own freehold ground. It was in a measure fulfilling the object for which it was erected. Services were held there regularly and every night the club was open for the youths and young men of the neighbourhood to avail themselves of, if they claw to do. He was thankful to say that the club had been attended with a fair me,lsure of SUCVSF.He would rather like t ) express the hope that their friends at St. Hilda'a would take great care lost the s•cular element should swamp the spiritual. The na,asion existed chit fly, of course, with the object of promoting the glory of God and the extension of the church et Christ, and they must keep that very much to the fore. Not only had they lengthened their cords in the way mentioned but they had also strengthened their stakes. He was very pleased with the marked increase in the attendances during the services of Lent, Holy Week, and he believed, Goo 4 Friday. He was present at the sr- vice on Good Friday etreoing and the attendance was very much greater than it had been since he came to the parish. The Easter services were particularly bright, and he trusted helpful, and the. number of com- municants had not been exceeded in any previous years of which he had any record and tie went back ten years For that he was thankful. He thought lee had a right to look for co-operatioe on the part of those who attended the services and he was grateful for such co- ,peration as bad been extended to him. He did not pretend, of course, to be satisfied, Far from it. He thought far more might be done by those who attended the services than had already been done. Still, for all the willing help he had received he was sincerely and deeply thank- ful. Hethanked his colleague,who since last Easter had worked hard in the parish as deacon and had rendered him very efficient and loyal help. He thanked Miss Burgess, who had helped him in very many ways in the parish,particularly in looking after the magszines,which during the last year had presented to them a very seb- stantial profit. From the wardens,sidesmen and counci I ' had received much loyal assistance and help. He ceuld not thank the lay readers too much fee their devoted work—work which was as efficient as it:was devoted.He might venture to say that few parishes could have better or more efficient ley helpers than they had at St James's, and the way in which they had conducted the Services at St Hilda's Mission was extremely geed. He alluded to Mr Stansfield, Mr Gibson, and Mr Westbrook They were always very reade to do all they could, and their whole heart was in the work which they did at the mission. The mission wardens ...were new to their work, but they were rendering every assistance towards making the mission the enemas they all hoped and prayed it might be. ora.aanviise and choir of both s's work was entirely the thankedehereh e vhedr mission.ym n c hr D the voluntary, and he devoted himielf very enthusiastically to the training of the choir who gave their Services at the mission. He thought the younger choir could, if anything, set the St James's choir an example in regu- larity of attendance. They knew proverbially that new brooms swept clean, but there had been time n ,av for the new broom to have lost a little of its novelty, and yet it continued to sweep clean very efficiently. Tbe St James' choir might be a little more regular in attend- ance and more particularly at practicer. He need not enumerate the other church workers, and yet he must mention the services of the day and Sunday school teachers. Speaking of the day schools reminded him of a work which was not often brought to the notice ot the public, a real and heavy work done by the corres- pondent of the day schools. They knew that Mr Burgess, who had managed the account• of the schools for so long, Stilt continued to render very generous and active assistance in this way. Then he must thank the Band of Hope, the Girls' Friendly Society and the megazine distributors. The last named had brought £l6 16s to the church funds. He thanked those who collected twice during the year—for the Walking Day fund and tea party, and he was grateful to the sub- scribers to those funds as also to the clergy fund subscribers and these who looked after the fund. Cern- , ing nearer to Easter Day. be thanked those who made their offerings during Lent, and those who brought them to be dedicated to the church on Easter Sunday. There were still a number of boxes out, and he hoped they had collected as large a sum as in any previous year. Lastly he would thank all in the non- gregation who were so kind as to make him the off 'r- lug they did on Easter Sunday. He appreciated their kindness very much indeed. He thanked them not only for that offertory but for the one on Christmas Day. They had helped in the bet year many funds he was thankful te say. They were not selfish in their offer- ings. He did not know that they could increase their help, but he would be glad if they could help the many inatitutions and societies which were always clamour:ns for assistance. They ha] helped not only their own sick and poor fund,the mission building fund and the day and Sunday schools, but the Infirmary, the Nursing Institution, the Diocesan Societies, the Diocesan House of Mercy, the Additional Curates Society, the Enelieh Church Union, the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel, the Waifs and . Strays Society, the Uni- versities' Mission to Central Africa, and the Jerusalem Mission. In conclusion the Vicar, in speaking of the election of officers, said he felt very strongly that they should elect men who were deeply in earnest in church work and who showed an intelligible interest in church life, not only by their attendance at the services of the church but also at any church functions which they might have.

He described his case and cure as follows: "In 1889 my first sickness started. It was rheumatism in the back, and I had it close on six months. I consulted a doctor four times. At the beginning of 1890 the doctor told me I wasn't fit for work. A friend had advised me to have vapour baths once a week, and for five weeks I took his advice. At first I felt much better, but after the fourth bath my appetite went, and after the fifth my back got worse. Then somebody told me to apply fomentations, which I did, but still suffered great pain. In a short time my ribs became bad, and

THE. CORPORATION TRAM NAYS ---- An Early Morning Trial Run.

In the early hours of Sunday morning the War- rington Corporation tramway roate from Sankey Bridges to Latchford was for the first time traversed by one of the handsome electric cars, and Warrington people will be gratified to learn that the trial was attended with complete success. Although strenuous efforts had been made to keep the time of the trial unknown to the public, it somehow leaked out that the car would leave the car-shed in Mersey-streat between midnight on Saturday and one o'clock on Sunday morning and the result was that quite a large crowd assembled , in Mersey-street to witness the departure. The car left the shed at a quarter past twelve, the passengers being Mr W H Grimadale, (electrical en- gineer and tramways manager to the Corporation) Mr Charman (representing the British Thomson Houstoa Company, who are responsible for the electric equipment), Mr Marsh (representing Messrs Preece andldCardew consulting engineers), and Councillor Jon Evans (one of the members of the Tramways Committee). Later these gentlemen were joined by Councillor Tinnion. Mr Charman steered the c r assisted at times by Mr erimsdale, and the fir6t run was to Sankey Bridges, and thence to Latchford Swing Bridge. The car traversed the lines several times until nearly six o'clock, when the trial, which was regarded as eminently sittisfac- tory, came to an end, about 15 miles having been covered altogether. Of course, there were frequent stoppages for purposes of inspection, all the "turn- outs" and cross-avers (points) coming in for especial examination. The overhead equipment was also found to be in good working o7der, alteaugh,as was to be expected, several minor alterations will have to be made before the service is in full swing. Great caution was observed on some parts of the route, but on others, when the track was clear, a high rate of speed was obtained. Eight of the cars have arrived and two more are on order for the „jVilderspoal route, the service on which will be peaed as soon as possible

Friendly Society Returns. The annual numerical returns of the Manchester Unity of Oddfellows, just issued, shows a total membership in all classes of 996,531 During the year the lodges in Great Britain and Ireland ad- mitted 43,639 new adult members ad the increase in the colonial and foreigu districis was 5,210. The society lost by deate 9,210 members by the seces- sion and expulsion of lodges 921 members. and by the secession of members 25.049. The total adult membership at the close of the year was 850,709, an increase of 13669. The juvenile branches admitted 18,124 new members, and transferred 8.210 to adult lodges. The total juvenile membership is 115,484. --Mr J Lister Stead, permanent secretary of the Ancient Order of Foresters has issued the numeri- cal and financial returns of the order for the year ended December 31st last. Tee returns show a total membership of 928,035, made up of male adult ben-fit members, 748,011; female adult members, 7.965; honorary members, 21,812; widows contri- buting for funeral allowances, 22,178; and juvenile members, 128,069 The net increase of members for the year is 13,452, of whom 12,813 are male adultlbenefit members. The juvenile membership shows a decrease of 1,714. During the year 40,993 persona were initiated as members of the adult order 9,098 members died, 24,723 members seceded, and 55 c.iurts, with 6,862 memoers. which had been suspended, were reinstated. The total funds amount to £7,172,833, a net increase of £337,022, the largest increase o.n record During the year sick pay has been paid to 182 639 ellfferent members in the United Kingdom, woo received an average allow- ance per member of 46.85 days, compared with 214,640, members and an average of 41.0 S days for , 1900. •

- TO CLOOGERS.—ON RATA, the WORKSHOP AND A- STOOK-IN-TRAMS of the late dward Rothsrell ; situated in Stanley-street--Apply 23, PARR BROW Tyldeeley Manchester.

Mr CIIAMIERLAIN has Prisoners of War, before him a matter of some difficulty in meeting an appeal which is to be made to him by the Aus- tralian Ministry. It is understood that Mr BAR- TON will be moved to move in turn the Colonial Secretary on behalf of Lieutenant WITTON of the Eushvelt Carbineers, who has just arrived at Southampton under sentence of penal servitude for life. Nothing in Erckmann-Chatrian's stories of the Napoleonic Wars, or, indeed, in modern melodrama, reads half so grim, so terrible, so dark with fate enveloping in its folds a bright young life as the lieutenant's tale. He is a young fellow with an open countenance, a strong, manly face, but unhardened by the campaign or by the prospect ot the Cimmerian future it seems to have made for him. In the height of the Imperial en- thusiasm at the Cape he volunteered for service, and it may be taken for granted he was a man of good character, otherwise he would not have been given a commission. Some of the Australian yeo- men who enlisted as ordinary troopers were pro- digal sons,but the selection of the officers was made very carefully. Toe lieutenant's account of his degradation is that Boers had shot several of the Carbineers while they were lying wounded on the ground,and that a senior officer gave orders for the shooting down of all Beers on whom hands could be laid. According to this statment, the young man is to suffer twenty years of privation and I sorrow because of his enthusiastic loyalty and 1 docile obedience So, at least, some of his apolo— gists will say. It is important, however, that the matter should be clearly understood. Aus- tralian yeomen in taking service were bound • by the ordinary rules of martial law, and the fact that they were inspired more by patriotism than pay avails nothing. The col- ditions are macie in. the mutual interests of all people that go to war. Further, the pies that the Boers who exhibited the white flag and were shot in the act were our enemies counts for nothing. They were by the proclama- tion of Lord ROBERTS fellow-subjects of the KINel, and to shoot them unless they were handling firearms, in which case they were rebels, must be an act of murder. Of course, if treachery were general among the Boers our soldiers would have to take a common-sense rather than a theoretical view of the situation, and not wait for the sound of bullets to ascertain whether the white flag was or was not a genuine demonstration. Bat these:eases of slimness have been an exception rathe than a rule. This view of the situation must applY with equal severity to Boer commandants who are caught after breaking the rules of the Geneva Convention. It would be preposterous if, for example, the evidence against Ken zixc;na be strong, to expect us to be sentimental over him and yet on reading that two Australian officers had been shot for infractions to say : "How just and proper." ,That would be carrying magnanimity too far. The Bible does not require us to love our neighbours, whether they be enemies or not, better than ourselves; and it is sufficient to take t that high ethical standard. There is, however, room for mercy while endea- vouring to maintain some decency on the battle- field. For one thing, despite the anomaly of the proclamations, our Government admits that practi- cally we are still in the midst of a real war, and not the mere flickering embers. The punishment for acts of inhumanity should rest on those primarily responsible; and if the explanation of Lieutenant WITTON, that he acted under order 9 from a senior, be true, then, of course, he should be released. But even if he were particeps crinzinis we may well be content,!allowing for the circular stances of passion, in making examples of more prominent officers who were associated with hirs. Their graves on the lonely veldt shew that we have dealt out judgment with an impartial hand, and are not respecters of persons. We may hope that the KING will be induced to shew clemency in thi9 instance, and that discipline will not suffer on thA account.

ONE of the uses of the After the Holiday. holidays—a use exer- cised quite unconsci- ously—is that they form settling days for punlic Opinion. Englishmen, according to the old pro- verb, take their pleasures sadly ; and although they welcome the breathing time from work, and may seem in many cases to indulge themselves pretty generously, most of them have some serious thoughts this Eastertide.Christmas and Easter are times of:family stocktaking, when the circles are made up in town or country, and fathers discuss with their sons, and brothers with brothers, as a change from sheer amusement, what are their prospects. That is characteristically English. It is only when an Englishman gets drunk that he quite forgets the serious business of life,and it may be taken for granted that in most households during the past few days there has been, as a break from the amuserneLts, an examination of prospects and a discussion on the questions of the future. The outlook cannot be regarded as bright for the rising generation, or for the large body of hand-to-mouth workers. So far the taxation from the war has had little appreciable effect, save to put old age pensions outside the reach of practical political politics. The burden has been very evenly distributed, and the people have borne it cheerfully, because they felt that Bach a course would assist the Government in the prosecution of the war. There can be no doubt also, that the employment of so many Reservists at the front has not been without its compensa- tions at home. The balance of ordinarily unem-

ployed have benfited ia being called in to fill the vacancies, and during the early part of the war, at any rate, there were comparatively few men out of work. The state of things is beginning to change. The increase in the rate of interest is beginning to tell on the specula- tive building trade, and bricklayers and brick- /avers labourers are already feeling the difference. It will affect the carpenters before long. Then in the course of a few months, as the Reservists are brought back, men in all kinds of positions will be dispossessed of their temporary places, and the patriotic services of our citizen soldiers will be remembered—at least,we may hope %o—by employers. On the other hand, however,it must be remembered that the Army is being made more attractive to the surplus male population,and the need for filling up the gaps will correct to some extent the overflow in business channels. Still, it is evident that life will be stressful for the mass of the population, and that the prosperity of the last decade is likely to be subject to a heavy discount in the near future. There is one other sign of the times which must cause sme inquietude. This is the tobacco war,with Its ominous suggestion of the development of trusts in this country. In the combat which has just begun between the groups of British an Atzierican firms, money is to be spilled much more copiously than is blood on the actual battlefield, and we are told that one or the other of the great leagues is to be crushed. :The hope of the fighters, of course, is that they may receup themselves out of the British public when the other side is obliged to retire worsted, and something like a monopoly is "established. Happily this first large experiment In trusts in Great Britain is made in a line in Which it should be most difficult to secure a mor o- Poly. The shops are the most easy to equip with stock-in-trade,and any attempt to manipulate. them O n one side or the other is almost certain to fail If the small tobacconists were to turn their shops into tied houses for the English or the Americans, other shops would be opened. The British work- ingman may also '3e trusted to shew some impar- tiality in this matter, and we may hope that the contest will be a warning rather than an encour- agement to other American concerns; but it will want watching, nevertheless.

The very unpleasant statement is made that there are as many illiterate men over twenty-one Years of age in the Southern States of America as there were half a century ago. The Public Educa- tion system has been working for more than thirty Years, and the number of schools is larger than the North Atlantic States can shaiv. Both these facts argue sheer indifference. It might have been thought that old feeling of resentment against the blacks would have operated as regards this matter le a useful direction. The wish to maintain , Superiority over the coloured races should have affected the lower classes sufficient to make them ' eager to be able to read and write. But it is est so. The unskilled labourers maintain the same indifference to opening up the ordinary channels for exercising intelligence. 7he negroes in a considerable proportion take advantage of schools; but generations must come Xld go,and hereditary characteristics must modify Defore they can use to much commercial profit the benefits conferred upon them. We hear occa- clonally of a public man such as Mr Washington ,1300ker, whom President Roosevelt delights to eenour, and like Dunbar the poet; but these bright, intellectual stars only deepen the feeling Of .disappointment as to most of the swarthy- skinned. So the indifference remains among the lower strata of the white population, and fifty , years have passed without any perceptible quicken- zing of them from inertia. Some people are dis- Posed to trace the failing to the poverty caused by he Civil War; .but it is sufficiently remote for its influence to be ended now.

Sir Walter Besant's Autobiography had been carried to its natural ending before the novelist died, and its issue at the present time comes as an interesting memorial of his career. Sir Walter regarded novel-writing as a profession rather than a vocation, and the creations of his fancy are not likely to have a lasting fame. They are pleasant easy, clean reading, but without much distinction of style. They were turned off with regularity on an average one a year for seventeen years,and they were sure of a market. Others have emulated Sir Walter—only, as the frishrlan would say, more so giving us books of entertainment at the rate of three or four a year. Perhaps the fact that " All Sorts and Conditions of Men" inspired the dove- ,lopment of the People's Palace led Sir Walter to ae satisfied quite early with work or a purpose; and, after all, is rnolesome fiction,. which banishes anxiety and brightens a passing hour, ..‘lid still rings true, altogether purposeless ? Sir 'alter, too, though he never pretended to havea kission, had a great sense of what was due to his nfession as an author. He never resorted to Indirect methods of advertising. He allowed his .toublishers to manage the " publicity" department. 'ln the whole course of his Autobiography he never Illentions the Knighthood which was conferred 11130 n, him by the late Queen Victoria. His modesty °r.loade that. In two directions outside novel- writing Sir Walter also did noteworthy service. books.on London were compiled with loving 4e!lre, and are delightful monuments of the tradi- Zians and history of the metropolis. His interest :a the Palestine Exploration Fund was very helpful to a movement of immense benefit to scholarship.

-- In the death of Mr. Rhodes the Empire has lost Igna of its greatest and most dominant men, while aouth Africa has been bereaved of he who has 14.°11,0 more than any other to mould its future des- The deceased statesman was great in the aenSe that his wealth was enormous, and his :aPacity for formulating and carrying out vast tct,ilemes, really colossal. He was not a man of "B Bright or Gladstone stamp, but one of those .wh° placed power and empire before principle.

In this respect Mr. Rhodes' patriotism, though sincere enough, was of a dangerous type, for to a great extent it was based on the assumption that territorial aggrandisement came easily first in all considerations for the Empire's greatness. He had a great respect for "territory "—the word was almost the guiding star of his existence—and very little for social and political ideals. The patriotism of the materialist was ,emphatically his, and it is this fact which I apparently impels the" Manchester Guardian" to make the following severe but not altogether mis- taken estimate of his life. "With a real inclina- tion to serve his country and with powers that would have enabled him to serve her effectually, the judgment of history will, we fear, be that he did more than any Englishmen of histime to lower the reputation and to impair the strength and compromise the future of the Empire."

Warrington has already decided to commemor- ate the Coronation of King Edward VII. in a number of ways. One item in the programme is the presentation of an illuminated address to His Majesty. We commend the following remarks of the "Local Government Chronicle" for the con- sideration of the local committee :—"We would earnestly impress upon local authorities" says this journal," the desirability of refraining, as far as possible, from showing their loyalty by means of unnecessarily expensive addresses, which will not afford the slightest gratification to any person except the senders, and which in all probability the King will never even see. At the time of her late Majesty's death we know for a fact that within a period of three days over 7,000 addresses were I received at the Home Office. The receipt of these was officially notified, but His Majesty the King certainly did not see one per cant of them,and the remainder might for all practical purposes have been sent direct to the waste •paper basket. If we take the average cost of each address at the low figure of three guineas we arrive at the astonishing and disappointing result that over £21,000 was spent in a period of three days which really pro- vided no satisfaction to anyone except the illum- inators and dealers in vellum. When we consider the amount of good which rc ight have been done had this large sum been applied in a more practical way we cannot but hope that a similar occurrence may he avoided on this occasion. We imagine that what we all want is to do that which, while showing our loyalty to and affection for our Sovereign, would be most pleasing to himself, and the natural ques- tion to ask is in what form the expression of our loyalty would be most acceptable to the King? It must be evident to everyone that the causes which he has most at heart are the amelioration of the condition of the poor, the sick. and the suffering, and in the promotion of works of this and a simi- lar description we shall do more to please him than can be done by presenting him with any numb3r of elaborate addresses, which he will certainly never read and probably never see."

Red tape is for ever clogging the wheels of progress and slowing their movement. We note that the Local Government Board has, on the ' report of its official auditor, elecided that the Warrington Rural District Council is not entitled to send a deputation to the Public Health Congress at the expense of the rates because such a congress is not deemed to come within the meaning of section 2 of the Public Health and Local Government Conference 1885. The absurdity of the thing is apparent, and we fail to see why the Local Government Board and its auditor cannot rest content as long as the spirit of the law is observed. If there is any annual conference where it is advisable such bodies as Rural and Urban Councils should be represented,the Health congress is the one. Coun- cillors have here an unequalled opportunity of learning the most up-to-date methods of preserv- ing the public health and profiting by the experience of other authorities. The Local Government Board's action is, as several mem- bers remarked, a distinctly retrogressive step, and one certainly not in accord with the wishes of those who framed and established the Act quoted. The protest which has been sent from Warrington is very necessary in the interests of the Council and of the ratepayers whom the Board seems so solicitous about, and we hope a reconsideration of the matter will result in a more commonsense view being taken of "the meaning of the Act." Otherwise, we shall be tempted to say that the Local Government Board, as well as the law " is a hass."

MATTERS OF MOMENT. A Provincial Letter.

No longer can it be claimed for any particular month ttat it is par excellence the month of Confer- @aces. Easter has duringlthe week asserted itself:as a popular and "convenient season" in this respect. For, while 801:120 have given themselves to pleasure and others to prayer and thanksgiving, the National Union of Teachers has been in conference at Bristol, the Rural Teachers' Federation held its annual meeting in the same city, the Independent Labour Party was in conference at Liverpool, and the Amalgamated Union of Shop Assistants, Ware- housemen and Clerks at Newcastle-on-Tyne, and the Midland Counties' Trades Federation at Stour- bridge, while the Good Tenn:dare held their annual gathering at Exeter. The opinions of a president of the National Union of Teachers naturally vary to a went ex ent, according as he happens to be a Voluntary or a School Board teacher, on the vexed question of which in England, owing to a national peculiarity, occupies the place of thought which educational efficiency would have elsewhere. But many men who are ordinarily opposed in politics were no doubt glad to see that, in dealing with the Education Bill of the present government, Sir Allen Croft joined in condemning the " local option" clauses of that measure. There seems to be a concanaus of opinion in the pro- vinces generally that this feature o! the Bill must disappear. It is a satisfaction to welcome the shop assistant as a conference holder. On Sunday trading.lhowever, the :conference gave an uncertain deltverancs. A motion in favour of Closing all shops in the dry goods trade by Act of Parliament was met by an amendment to prepare a Bill for the compulsory closing of all shops on Sun- day in Scotland ; but it is a vain dream that the legislature will pass an Act to declare a Sabbatarian offence on one side of the Tweed that which is per- missible on the other. Equally inconsequent were one or two protests that were raised, on the "living in" or " out ' system, &c, which matters the men have already in their own hands if they choose to exercise their existing rights. All the same, the conference can ba said not to have been held in vain.

Unhappily, the holidays have not passed without one or two shocking and fatal accidents, and a disastrous collision in the English Criminal. In the very early hours of Tuesday morning, in thick weather, with driving rain, the Landon and South- Western Company's steamer Alms collided with the Cambrian Princess, belonging to Liverpool, nine miles off the Nab Lighthouse. The latter vessel, the smaller of the two, received the impact amid- ships, and was so severely injured that she sank in about four minutes, eleven lives being lost, number- lug exactly half the crew.

It did not take up much of the thus of the House e of Commons, previous to adjourning for the holiday, to give a second reit iing to the Shop Clubs gill. Pe Shop Club is a comparatively new institution, and, as pointed out in this column a few weeks since, it is complained by the ad- coCates of the measure, and by .the supporters of the friendly societies, that it is an innovation un- favourable to the welfare of the majority of the men and women who hold memberships. Among the reasons adduced for this hostile attitude is the one that Shop Clubs have hitherto eicap 3d registra- tion' and are therefore not amenable to proper supervision also; that the membership is rendered compulsory at the will of the employer, who may deduct the subscription from the wages of the employ 6 ; that membership does, not last longer than the retention of the emplo3 6 8 service at a par- ticular establishment, and that, therefore, he for- feits the accrued beneits by making a change ;and, finally, that the members of such ship cubs nave no control over the funds which they help to create. Other arguments are urged by the suppor- ters of the measure, which has been described by one critic as " A Bill For the protection of friendly societies,"since the Shop Club of the usual type performs a function that is antagonistic to the

work of these great provident association and might eventually undermine their status. The National Conference of Friendly Socie- ties, representing millions e working men and women, some time since passed a resolution con- demning the establishment of shop clubs, as an interference with the liberty of the subject. If the compulsory memberships were rendered illegal, however, and registration were enforced by statute. the shop club might be made to encourage thrift among certain classes of workers rather than other- wise The House of Commons on rising for the holidays adopted the view that the Shop Clubs Bill embodied the principle of a useful measure; but the Government indicated that while it would not oppose the second reading, the measure will have to be considerably amended in detail, during the Com- mittee stage. The Friendly Societieir,may thus far be assumed to have gained a victory.

When a Royal Commission like that on Alien Immigration is appointed, the Govennment of the day can be congratulated first, that they thereby postpone discussion on a subject which they are more or less pledged to deal with, and, secondly, that they secure information and advice which may be of value should they ever decide to legislate thereon. It is not usual for a Government to refer even a complicated problem to a Royal Commission if it has any desire or intention to take the matter up in the immediate future, and it so rarely happens that Ministers act upon the recommendations of Com- missions of inquiry that the public look upon them all with some amount of suspicion. Lord Salisbury has before now recognised their political value, and Commissions have not by any means escaped his gibes. But the matter is referred to here as being one of special interest and importance to industrial people in the provinces, and it may be presumed that if the Prime Minist-r did not think some practical good would follow, he is not quite the one to readily consent to the appointment of such a commission. Of course, the opposition will be at perfect liberty, in commenting upon this new in- quiry, to suggest that it was Lord Salisbury who asked the Liberals to pass an Alien Immigration Bill which he introduced himself during Lord Rose- bery's administration, although he has failed to bring the matter forward during the years he and his party have been in office. It goes without say- ing, that all politicians attempt much when in Opposition from undertaking which they shrink when in power, and the appointment of this Royal Commission may in reality be taken as an admission on the part of his Majesty's advieers. that the ques- tion is not ripe for settlement. That Lord Salisbury should have thought it was, more than ball a dozen years ago, is another story.

The better the wages, the more drink: the more drink, the more crime. Such was the melancholy, but, it is feared, true sequence put before the meet- ing of the Discharged Prisoners' Aid Society in the West of England recently. The returns showed a substantial increase of the number of persons com- mitted during the year, and the fact was explained in the way stated. In prosperous times the prison population increases; in times of distress, when work is less and wages are low, it diminishes. The more money plople have the more they spend in drink, and drunkenness is the main cause of crime. Some twenty years ago there was a wonderful tem- perance revival in the principal city of the West and a newspaper representative from one of the furthermost parts of the district journeyed to Bristol to " investigate " the matter. The Chief Constable of the city said," Yes ; it is remarkable how many people have lately turned teetotalers But you wait until they get better wages again ! " It was a time of depression, and the enterprising journal of the West that sent its representative to institute an inquiry at the time,now fears that with the return of prosperity the people immediately begin to squander their money in excessive drink- ing, The question remains, therefore, what is to be done? Teachers and philanthropists will no doubt continue to do what they can to train children and young persons in habits of prudence and self-con- trol, but the great difficulty appears to be with the older people.
