"RELIGIOUS THOUGHT IN INDIA." --- Lecture by the Rev. C. H. Hocken.

The last of the winter " club nights" in connection with the Warrington Society was held in the Old Academy, ou Wednes fay evening. A lecture ots " Rc- ligious Thought in India." was delivered by the Rev C H Roemer), of Bev:sly-road Wee!even Church, who for 12 years was a missionary in the Mysore district. The chair was occupied by Mr W H J Jenkins, who briefly introduced the lecturer. At the outset, the lecturer traced the settlement of the Hindu branch of the Aryan family in the Punjab 4,000 years ago. In order to make clear the course of religious thought during these 4,000 years, the lecturer said he Wended to divide this time into three periods, the Vedic, the Buddhist and the Hindu periods. The Vedic period was that in which the hymns, institutions and ritualism of the four Vedas bad their origin and were more or less supreme in the religious and social life of the people This period lasted from 2,000 to 500 B.C. At the beginning of this period they found no trace of the pre- sent system of idol worship ex,sting among the early settlers of the Punj ih. In the hymns of the Vedas they worshipped the phenomena of nature. Thesystem of caste, which at first was evolved by the necessities of society hardened in the hands of the unscrupulous Brahman priests into an unbearable system of tyranny. During this period the Ary in settlers established coloaiee and made thsmselves masters of the whole of Northern India and prosperity and leisure destroyed their primi- tive simplicity. Faith in the old Vedic gods decayed. They did net satisfy man's thirst for knowledge nor did they throw sufficient light on the unbearable mystery of the future. Daring sceptics arose and various rationalistic syeems of philosophy were founded. Each school professed to explain' the Vedas tut these metaphysical speculations killed the primitive faith and culminated in a System ofy pantheism—a system which teaches that there is only one entity or substance in the universe. This dreare creed could not touch the imagination nor satisfy the heart of the common people; they bad no religion to live by and groaning under the tyranny of the Brah- man priests they Were in a Oates of insurrection against the old order of things. Society was ripe for a new epoch. About 500 B.c. Buddha was born and with him began the Buildniat period and a new era in the history of religious thought in India. The history of Buddha was sympathetically traced by toe lecturer:The message of Buddha which he formulated by his unaided reason was summarised by the lecturer as being that there was no God in the world higher than man ; no bell butrtie misery of hbznan Ili?, and no heaven but annihilation. Many causes led to the spread of Buddhism. The Vedic religion was dead ; the people were in Insurrection against the extravagant claims to ecclesias- tical authority on the part of the Brahman priests. Buddhism sprung out of the past as did Christianity out lof Judaism. It was the Vedic religion popularided and adapted to the peychological moment. The poorest and ignorant were captivated by its tenderness and sympathy with all forms of suffering ; with its doctrine of the brotherhood of man and exemption from caste. The moralist was no less attracted by the beauty and purity of its ethics and philosophers by the subtilty of its metaphysics. In 500 years, that was about the com- mencement of the Christian era, the Buddhist faith wail everywhere tiiumphant. With the beginning of to Christian era began the Hindu period, the third period in the history of r3ligious thought in India. At that time the Buddhist wave of enthusiasm in India had spent its force, prosperity had opened the way for cor- ruption and relaxed the strenuous character essential to successful existence. And the Brahmans, never exterminated, rallied their forc.s to the etack. They re-established the authority of the Vedas, but interpreted them on the lines of the Buddhist philosophy and incrroorated into their system the popu- lar forrns of worship current among the Aryans throngh their association with the primitive tribes. Hinduism became a huge amorphous system held together by the doctrine 'of pantheism and it had gradually displaced Buddhism altogether. The religious ideas of Hinduism were r3ferred to at length by the lecturer, who pointed out that it led to a popular system of idolatry. The idols were various forms of minor gods, who adminis- tered the various departments of hula in affairs. The common people regarded the idol as actually and liter- ally God himself and by their worship and offerings considered that they had placed God under an obliga- tion to help them. The course of religious thought in India had been steadily downward, so that the last state was much worse than the first. It had lost itsl primitive simplicity and purity. Christianity had in- ' tervened in India to arrest this downward movement. Christian ideas were permeating religious thought in India and influencing all classes of society. Already there were three millions of Christians in our Indian empire, and the work of evangelisation compa-ed favourably with the progress of Christianity in the Roman empire. Hinduism had no future before it and there was no reason to doubt that in India as in other parts of the world the future belonged to the Lord Jesus An interesting discussion ensued, in which reference was made to the simil %city lostween some of the religious ideas taught by Buddha and those of Christianity. The lecturer's services were acknowledged on the proposition of Mr SMITH, seconded by Mr H M Asuroar. SPRING. JACKBTS. New designs and shapes. latest Novelties. NOTES ON UAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

By " Ovalist."

Warrington were busy last week end playing the game instructor-general to the small fry of the Northern Union. On Friday they beat Birkenhead Wanderers,and the following day South Shields were taken in and gently done for,Fish perforraing the feat of the match by scoring after a run nearly,the length of the field,and converting the try. Monday saw Warrington at home to their old foes Wigan, who came to fulfil the first of their fixtures in the South West Lancashire Competition. They also went down after a hard struggle, the margin in favour of Warrington being 10 points to 5 points.

Just now the South West Lancashire Competition could not be in a more interesting state, for Widnes and Warrington are running neck and neck for the cup and medals. Until last Friday Warrington seemed cer- tain of the honour, but on that day Runcorn fairly bowed the knee to their neighbours, who had a soft thing on in the shape of Runcorn " A "instead of the premier organisation. Of course the Seniors were being saved for the following day's match at Castleford, and there were a few smiles outside Runcorn when it was found that Friday's expedient had only resulted in the loss of both matches. Widnes have Leigh at Widnes yet to play, and as they defeated the Leighites away they should have no difficulty at Lowerhouse-lane. Still, even St. Helens effected a surprise. Warrington's chance depends entirely on the result of their match at Wigan to-day.

Wigan were determined on Monday to make a bold bid for the cup and over a thousand enthusiasts travelled with the team from the salubrious colliery district and they had no hesitation in showing on which side their sympathies lay. When their favourities entered the enclosure, looking very smart in cherry and white jerseys,there was a shout of delight and through- oat the match, the Wigan fifteen was the object of many encouraging remarks. Undoubtedly, Wigan have done extremely well in the second league competition and also lathe South West Lancashire League. I was pleased to hear that they have been well supported Ithis season and that at to-day s match with Warrington they will probably have a crowd of something like seven thousand on the Wigan ground. It is a little surprising that such near neighbours have not met each other earlier •in the season and that both home and away matches should be arranged to take place within 1 a week.

Both teams were representative. Edmonds was tried at centre in the Warrington three-quarter line but it cannot be said that the experiment was a great success A. little more avoirdupois is necessary for a vigorous centre three-quarter, especially when, as was the case on Monday, the Warrington forwards are overpowered by a superior eight. The Wigan forwards proved their superiority in weight and staying power and gave their backs opportunities, which if they had come Warring- ton way, would have been made better use of.

Playing with the wind behind them in the first half, Warrington had the advantage and Harris very speedily added another try to his rapidly growing list. No goal resulted, but Fish made amends shortly afterwards by kicking a penalty goal. Dickenson was mainly re- sponsible for Warrington's second try. He made a zig- zag run through a host of opponents but was eventu- ally collared, Hockenhall picked the ball up from the ground and ran behind the posts, without any opposi- tion being offered by the Wigan players. There was a howl of indignation from the Wiganites when the point was allowed. it :being the general opinion that Dickenson was tackled before parting with the ball. The chagrin of the visitors was e nsiderably heightened when Fish added a goal. When the teams turned round, the Warrington team was leading by ten points to nil.

The Wiganers played up with great dash in the second half, the for wards monopolising the scrimmages and herling out repeatedly. The backs were given numerous chances but poor use was made of them, Nelson beiig the only player who did himself justice. At length, Ball, one of the forwards, scored under the posts and Nelson added a goal. The visitors strove strenuously to get on level terms and but for over- eagerness they might have done so. Warrington were penned in their own quarters for most of this half, and the call for no time came as a relief to the home sup- porters. Townson and Hockenhall worked well together n the half-back division but they were not given many opportunities to shine on Monday,. except in tackling, and Hockenhall maintained his reputation in this respect. Fish had very little to do, but on one or two occasions he gave the visitors a taste of his quality. Hallam played an excellent defensive game. .On the Wigan side, the palm must be given to the forwards. Nelson and Bothwell were the only backs who played first-class football.

The South-West Lancashire League table up-to-date is as follows : *

Points L. D. For Agst. Pte.

Warrington have selected the following team to do duty against Wigan ; Hallam, full back; Fish, Hocken- hall, Dickenson and Harris, threequarters ; Burgess and Allen, half-backs; Morrison, Boardman, J Swift,W Swift, Edmondson, Eden,Roberts and Forster,forwards.

Burgess must be thankful that his suspension is up for he has had to play the part of spectator while some of the best matches of the season have been played. Fortunately he has kept in training, and if he has not lost his form the Warrington back division should be far more effective this Saturday than it has been of late. Burgess is the best half we have for getting clear of the scrimmage. The forwards should also be able to give a good account of themselves if they use what weight and strength they have to the fullest extent.

The " A "team will entertain Stockport "A," and the following fifteen have been selected to do duty : Knight, full back; Illidge, Antrobus, Edmonds and Clare, three-quarters ; Townson and Timmins, half- backs; Cunningham, Anders, Aldred,Purslow, Higham, Baxter, Tayl R. and Jolley, forwards.

THE ALLEGED CHILD MURDER AT WIDNES.

Magisterial Inquiry.

At Widnes, on Tuesday, before Mr W A Gnasage, CatherineV helm], a young woman, living at 53, Wil- liam-4reet, Widnea, was charged with causing the death of her infant son James by ()rowing him in a dollytub on March 20. Mr Koowles (Widnes) defended, Superin- tendent KAghley narrated the facts of the case briefly, and stated that the prisoner had been committed by the coroner's jury on a charge of wilful murder. The charge was read over El the pi isoner and the cu tomary caution and admonition having been given, she was asked if she had any reply to make. In a weak yoke, low and scarcely audible, she said " The voices told ms tis do it. Tbe voices were on the stairs." Thee askod if she wished to say anything further she said she did not, making the reply with more firmness.

Several witnesses were ea led by Mr Knowles for the defence, their evidence being destined to show that the prisoner had been peculiar in her manner for a long Joseph Whelan, the husband (c Med by Mr Knowles). said he admit'ed striking his wife and quarrelling with her the night bof ore and morning after the child wai drowned. Oa the day of the inquest he was sitting next to her in the court, when she turned round to him and said " How are the children ? And the baby— where is it ?" A midwife n”iled Ekes stated that she saw prisoner at the police station. Sie askei her some very strange , questions, and seemel quite unconscious as to her posi- tioa, andlunable to appreciate the differenee being the ! child's being dead or alive. Several other neighbours were called, who spoke of the prisoner's strangenesi for some months past. Mary Casey, sister of the prisoner,said she complained to her that her husband and his mother were driving her mad. OnG, day since the prisoner had been in the police station she turnec' round to her and said, "It was not my child I drowned, but the devil's." Prisoner was committed to the assizes.

A PROFITABLE TRANSACTION.

On Tuesday at the Runcorn Police Court, a bey aged ten named John Bushell, Halton-road, was charged with stealing a silver watch and chain valued at 103 belonging to John Boden.—The evict- ence showed that the prisoner, a cousin of the pro• secutor, went to Boden's house in Bentinck-street and stole the watch and chain afterwards selling it to a small boy named William Cannington aged ten for the sum of one penny.—Cunuington in turn sold the watch to another by named John Riley for the sum of is 6,1 retaining the chain for himself On information being given to the police P. C, Bratt apprehended the prisoner who acknowledged the theft,—Prisoner was sentenced to receive four strokes of the birch. INTERESTING PRESENTATIONS AT RUNCORN.

At Lea's Temperance Hotel on Wednesday even- ing Mr Thomas Jones, manager of the Runcorn White Lead Works was presented with a beautiful marble clock and bronzes to mark the occasion of his marriage.—Most of the workmen were present and after a most substantial repast had been served up Mr George Rutter senior at the request of Mr Arthur Cropper made the presentation speaking in high terms of Mr Jones.—Mr Rosenberg secretary, also eulogised Mr Jones —Mr Jones, who was greeted with cheers, reiponded in a felicitous manner thanking them all for their beautiful pre- sent. The clock bears the following inscription on a silver plate:—"Presented to Mr. Thomas Jones by the employes of the Runcorn White 1... ad Company, Ltd , on the occasion of his marriage, Easter, 1902." The gifts were supplied by Mr Gilbert Pritchard, High-street, Runcorn. At the works of the Highfteld Tanning Company an interesting presentation took place on Thursday night when Mr R Johnson was made the recipient of a handsome marble clock and bronzes to mark his approaching marriage. Mr R H Posnett, on behalf of the employ6s, made the presentation re- ferring in glowing terms to Mr Johnson's services during his eleven years connection with the works Mr Johnson suitably responded. The clock bore the following inscription :—" Presented to Mr R Johnson, by the employes of the Highfield Tanning Company Limited, Runcorn, as a token of esteem on the occasion of his marriage, 2nd April, 1902."

THEFT FROM I TILL.

At the Runcorn Police Court on Tuesday, a Wid- nes man named Frederick Ford alias Cross was charged with stealing 7s, the money of Michael Conway from a beerhouse till in church-strest.— Prisoner was also charged with being in the" Welsh Harp," Cooper-street with intent to commit a felony and was further charged with doing wilful dam- age to a police cell to the value of 43.—The evi- dence showed that on Wednesday morning at 7 a.m. prisoner went into prosecutor's besrhouse and called for a drink. While the barmaid's attention was directed in another direction prisoner leaned over the bar and opened out the till. The girl, glancing rouod, saw prisoner with his hand in the drawer and raised an alarm. He at once ran away but was afterwards apprehended, the sum ot 78 being missed out of the drawer. On inquiries being made respecting the prisoner's whereabouts it transpired that the prisoner at 6.30 in the same morning had visited the " Welsh Harp " in Cooper-street and hood made his way inside the bar and opened the till, while the land- lord, Mil:Brown, went into the kitchen for some change. Prisoner ran away when the landlord asked him what he was doing. On prisoner being taken to the station, be behaved in a very violent manner, and smashed some woodwork in the cell in which he was confined.—P.C. Gregory deposed that the prisoner stated in reply to the charge that he would have taken more money toad he not bem seen.—Prisoner now pleaded not guilty.—On the first charge prisoner, who had a long list of convictions by the Widnes police, was sentenced to two month's hard labour and one month each on the other charges. HavraN PA.arsa MEETING.

The annual parish meeting was held on Thursday evening at the village hall, Mr Moores presiding.— The Clerk submitted the account of the St Thomas' ICaaritv. which showed receipts amounting to £2B Is 6d, and disbursements on St Thomas' Day of £25, leaving a balance of £3 191 6d.—lklr Woodier stated that during the past year there had been ten meet- ings of the Parish Council,besides several committee meetings. The Councl had given attention to the repairs of footpaths. and had put the cemetery in good order. The fees received on account of inter- ments, &c, amounted to £l7 7s 6d. They carried forward a balance from last year of £4 14s 8a and issued a precept in October for £l2,and another on Illonday night for £27, making total receipts of £6l 78 2d. The payments absorbed £56 Sc 9d. The cemetery had been beautified,and put in good order since the place had been opened there had been 235 interments, and the average for the last three years was 58. The Chairman referring to the gift by Sir John Brunner of the hall in which they were met, said the actual conveyance of the building to the Coun- cil had not yet been comple:ed, but be expected the title would be on the same lines as the Guild Hall at Runcorn. If that was so, they might he very well satisfied with it. He hoped the Parish Council would, in the near future, summon a special parish meeting to consider the question of adopting the Libraries Acts. lie would also like to see the adoption of the Lighting Act. He thought they wanted to be moving a bit. Other parishes were progressing in this direction and were doing very welLand he thought it was high time they bestirred themselves and went in for the improvements he had mentioned. The enormous expense had, in the past, been an obstacle to the founding of free libraries,but it was no longer necessary that a boon of this kind shoeld mean a heavy expenditure. If they only made up their minds he did we., antici- pate any difficulty in raising the necessary funds.

THE MANAGEMENT OF THE COTTAGE HOSPITAL.

The Chairman on behalf of the Cottage Hospital Com- mittee moved that the Council take over the ownership and control of the Cottage Hospital when camel( ted and free from debt and further moved that this Council agree to take over such ownership and control. fielsaid that the matter bad been !before the town from ten to Sfrden years and had occupied the time and the attention of succeeding chairmen of the C many of whom had gone to a good deal of pains that the sobj?ct at some future time might be not only a dream but a practical realizition of the wishes and hopes of a great many pe vie in the town. They were now within measurable distance of the scheme which was bearing fruit after all the Mears of past years, It gave him personally great pleasure to be associated in any way with the scheme which was for the advancement, the well being and the best interests of the town. During the past week or two they had succeeded in obtaining a site which was one of the best in the town °tamely, a site at the top of the Holloway. The trustees of the late Mr Chadwick had dealt very reasonably with the Cottage Hospital Committee and had placed at their disposal the top corner, about 2,500 square yards of groune,which itlwas estimated would be amply sufficieut for all the requirements of the hospital. The hospital wou'd face due south so that it w-u d get as much sun and light as possible.lie would like to call their attention to the way in which the funds of the cottage hospital had been got together. It was, perhaps, in the history of the town cize of the most remarkable instances of co—operation that they had ever seen. At any rate that he bad ever seen during his association with the town. Contributions and peemises of contributions had been given from large firms Ike the Manchester Ship Canal Company through the kindness of their friends Mr We wall and Mr Congreve who had interested themselves in the matter. That was but oee instance of large works rendering assistance and others might be given. They had also in the contributions one of £lO3 from the Runcorn Co-operative Soliety which instanced how the working classes in the town were thoroughly in sympathy with the movement. There was also a large contribution from the cycle club, a further in. stance of hearty enthusiasm. The hospital committee was formed during the year Mr Wiswall took the chair of the Council, and presiding at a meeting a'; the Technicl Institute the gentlemen present formed themselves into a Committee with power to add in order to bring to fruition the hopes of those interested in the project. Since that time the scheme had been making steady and slow progress The Committee wished that the hospital when budt should be founded on toe broadest basis possible, and that there shou'd not be room in any mind to think that it was a parte hospital or belonging to any section of the town—r Ws or poor, strong or weak. It was desirable that the Committee of Management should be in some way °teeny!. And ' as that Council was thus nyte qualified than any othcr body the Hospital Committee hoped the Council would see its way clear to take the deepest possible intereat in itlby putting this project on the broadest basis. With respect to the financial part they would see from the resolution that the committee did not desire the tows te take upon itself any burden with regard to the cost of the hospital. Toe Committers purpose Ito hand It over absolutely free. 'The purchase ground was free- hold and it was believed that sufficient support would be given to make the scheme" a thing of beauty and a joy forever." They had also gone carefully into the estimates and figures bad also been received from other authorities'and cottage hospitals. The cost of maintenance could not be more than £220 to £230 per annum. That mount the cenotnittee were perfectly sanguine of re- ceiving from the people of the town who bad so notly and generously contributed to the rearing of the hospi- tal. They regarded it as the child of the town and on that reason it was sure to be supported ha, its parents. The Ship Canal Company had promised £5O per MIR= for three years and there was no reason to imagine they would not continue to contribute towards Seen a worthy object.

lidr Taylor seconded. Mr Imison observed that the last time the matter came before the Committee statements were made as to the control, ownership and management, of some hospitals in the adjoining districts. It would be inter- esting if that information were now laid before them. The Chairman replied the only information he could Rive them WIN that a letter had been received from Mr Cooke be Mr Lake in which two hoapitals were men- tioned, Nortbwich and Winston]. The hospitals were vested in trustees and were maintained entirely by private subscription except that Northwich Council ccntribnted ten guineas and Winsford seven guineas which was all the money that came out of the rates. Mr Wiswall said be did not feel quite free to vote on the resolution and yet he did not like to move an amend- ment. The question was whether it was likely that the eort of maintenance would c)ine on the rates. Ile could never agree to it becoming • tax on the rates. Who could guarantee that it would not ? If its es'at Leh- ment and endowment were guaranteed they would adopt the resolution quite readily. The chairman would be well advised if he would defer the resolution at this early stage of the movement. Fie felt as if he was on thin ice. He did not at all feel at liber:y to vote on the resolution. Mr Handley suggested that the mater be referred to a Committee. There were two sides to the question. It should belong to the town, but there was also the fee!- ing that its cost should to provided for by private con- tributions. Both things could be accomplished, but at present they were at a stage when they could not say one thing or the other. It the matter were reared ta a Committee they would be in a better position to vote on the qsestitio. HA thought faith things could be accom- plished. It could be made into a public institution coming on the rates. The Chairman said he was quite willing to meet them on the matter and after futtner discussion it was re- sclord that the matter be lefi in the hands of a corn- mittes who would confer with the Cottage Hospital Committee and report to the Council in due course.

PADGATE.

CHRIST CHURCH. —The great Easter festival was fittingly celebrated at this church. The edifice had beeu decorated with much taste, and the effect was charm- ing. The bright spring fl•wers—the arum lily, daffodils, tulips, jonquils, and sarcissi—which lend themselves so effectively to the work of decoration, bad been lavishly eenployed. The pulpit, which had been decorated by Mrs W Elairia-Wright and Miss Jackson, was especially pretty, while the font r.ad been very tastefully decora- ted by Mrs J Douglas Buckton. The altar, choir stalls, and lectern, were beautifully decorated by Mrs Pollock- Hill, assisted by the Misses Birtles. The services throughout the day were of a bright and hearty charac- ter. In the morning Simper's anthem, " Christ our Passover," was well rendered by the choir, the tenor solo being sung by Mr W Grounds with great taste. The evening anthem was " King of kings," by the same compose r.the soprano solo being sung very sweetly by Master Edward Scholz. The Vicar preached at both services. Holy Communion was administered at 8 •a.m. and mid-day, the number of co nmunicants being larger than in previous years. Notwithstanding the fact that about £7O has treen raised in the past four weeks for the purpose of re-decorating the church, the offertories of Sunday constitute a record, being the largest on Esser Day since pew rents were abolished in 1897.

SOCIAL EVENING.—On Monday evening Mr and Mrs Blackwell entertained the pupils of toe Evening Con- tinuation School to a tea and social. An eiu•ellent repast was served to upward of fifty pupils and friend', and after the room had beta cleared the evening was spent in a pleasant manner.. Several games were indulzed in, " ping-poug " especially finding many enthusiaitic players. The Padgate Nigger Minstrels gave a ?apical " turn," and, in addition to a number of amusing irems of pitter,, songs were rendered by the troupe and by Messrs F Botrell, 8 Critchley, C Leech, A Faulks, and F -Ascher. Tier duet "What are the wild waves saying?" was sweetly sung by Miss D Weston and Miss E Pr3ll tds Mt Joseph Pass played a couple of piccolo solos and Mr W Grounds sang " The little Hero" in good style. Miss Dora Wes- ton rendered Sullivan's "Birds of the Night" very sweetly and Miss Edith Grounds Was heard to geod effect in Gounoes "To tbe Woods." During the even- ing Mr Blackwell (headmaster) gave a resume of the work of the session, and then called upon Mr G Mather.local representative on the Technical Instruction Committee, to distribute the prizes. In doing so, Mr Mather pointed to the value of a good education and advised the students not to miss any opportunity for the acquisition of knowledge. Tho prizes were awarded to those students who had attended 90 per cent or over of the possible number of hours, and were .won by Mary A Critchley, Enid Mellor, George lilass,} Harry Smith, John Richard Turner, Lewis Wis,fpn,lT Daintdth, F Weston, 8 Critchley, 0 Leech, glepla 'Pass, George Pritchard, T Potter, David Bibhy vd Plitt. A hearty cheer was then given for Mr and ,Mrs Blackwell for their kindness, and the proceedings terminated.

CONTAGIOUS EPIDEMIC.

Sometimes from sudden relapse, or contagious epidemic, the system becomes enfeebled, and nerves are unstrung. THE REV. E. R. TARBUCK AT ST GEORGE'S CHURCH, ALTRINCHAM.

The services at St George's were conducted by the Rev & R Tarbuck. At the close of his sermon in the morn- ing Mr Tarbuok said he desired to say how grateful he was for the kindness shown towards him. Sore in heart as they were at the departure of Mr Lutener, who had become so dear to them, lee should ever remember with gratitude their evident desire to welcome him and to give him encouragement in a position which,they would easily understand, was a most embarrassine one to him, and one which he felt most acutely. On all hands whether from an officer of the church or from a child,be had solar, received nothing but a smile of welcome and the encouraging grasp of the hand. On all essential points they would, no doubt agree, but they would not ask for a metaphorical looking-glass either in the pulpit or elsewhere. They did not want a man without opini- ons of his own, else would a talking automaton suit them, because then they could turn it on merely to re- flect their own ideas. He took it that they preferred a man who would try and teach them plainly the whole message of Jesus, . and yet try very hard never to be cruel out of cmelessness, or thoughtlessness, or want of sympathy. In a pariah like that there were many varieties of mind and many different conceptions as to the best way of bringieg eouls to Christ ; but he felt cur i that while they would prefer a man from whom they could agree to diffr, honouriog each other's inde- pendence in mind, striving not so much to wic in wordy , argument as to vie with each other in doing what was acceptable to the Lord. In this essential he felt sure I they would have unity, but in things th it were not essential let them not only expect but agree to differ. In all things let them take care that there should be charity. In the evening Mr Tarbuck said he fully appreciated the grief of the congregation at parting with Mr Lutener. But let them remember, it would not be needful by one jot or tittle to lessen their loyalty to Mr Lutener for them to give their affectionate loyalty to him. He came from a people who had the same feel- ings towards him that they had to Mr Lutener. Let them be patient with each other. They could not have the same personalities as his people at Warrington. He coed not,however much he agreed with their lat,. Vicar have quite theme personality, but he had had many talks with Mr Lutener before and eiece he accepted the living, and he thought he might sari that he knew his mind well, and in no point did he differ from him. Ho proposed to tees up his work as he would have it taken up. and to make it grow (tie the lines he would have it had he remained. He did him the honour of wiNhing him and persuading him to accept the living, and ho would try to be worthy of that honour. If he earned his approval in Fix mouths' time Mr Lutener would come over to give them a Send iy when he trusted that neither the congregation ncr he as Vicar would be ashamed to see him and to make their report.

BEFORE the MAGISTRATES

SMART PITNI9HMENT FOR ROBBING A SLOT METER. At the iVarriugton Police Court on Saturday before Mr T Eirchall. in the chair and other magistrates. Thomas Monks, carter, WAR charged with stealing Ss 4d from a gas slot meter in tiie house of John Garnett. 23, Robson-street, on Tuesday. Hepleaded not guilty. The Chief Constatle stated that the prisoner, although an able-bodied man had not done any work for a considerable time, having lived on the bounty of his married sister. Mrs Garnett. On Thurs- day Mrs Garnett went out,leaving no one in the house which was left locked up and when she was returning she saw the prisoner at the end of the street. When asked if he had been in the house, he replied that he could no get in, as the house-door was locked Wen Mrs Garnett went into the house she found an entrance had been effected by the removal of a piece of cardboard from one the windows at the back. It was subsequently ascertained that the slot-meter had been broken open and the contents —about 8s 4d—taken out. Later in the day, a boy named James Barton was given a letter by the prisoner to take to Mrs Garnett, and it ran as follows :—.• I have been in the house, and I have taken he 4d out of the box Doa't give Information to the police: I will 'etch it back on Saturday or to-night." Prisoner was arrested by Detective Meacock and Police-constable Hodgkiss. Evidence having been given prisoner, who had a bad record, was sent to gaol for three months. the Chairman remarking that he hsd acted in the meanest possible way.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Wycliffe PS.. Society.

' Owing to the holidays the attendance at the meeting of tais society on S.lCaly was not as large as usual. Mr Healey presided and remarked that the Easter season I was rally more important to them as followers of Christ than any other season of the year. That day they rejoiced to celehiwea the re ,urrec-ion of Christ which was the foundation of their hopes as Christians for the future life. Mr Whiteside,B.A., of Manchester,dclivered the address. There was only one subject, he said, which could be spoken about at a meeting of that kind. and that was the resurrection of Christ. The eyes of all Christian people were turned that day towards one place and one central figure The speaker proceeded to show that Christ's life on earth was an exemplification of:a new prineiple which He laid down, the principle of self-sacrifiee.selt-denial, gf abasement, self-crucifixion in the interests of other people. It was neceesary, the speaker continued, for Christians to morn thoroughly appreciate the self-denial of Christ and endeavour to follow Christ's example. They were followers of a living Sairear and if they en- deavoured to be true to their Christian principles they might depend upon it that they would receive the help they needed to do en.—Miss Bridge sang with taste and expression two solos entitled" Entreat me not to leave thee," and " I know that my Redeemer livetb."—Mr Payton, Mue Bac, presided at the organ —Mr Farrar, the hen secretary, referred to the tact that two mem- bers of the PS A would shortly depart for the front. On behalf of the society he had written them wishing them " God speed." He had received a reply from one of the men, Mr Mark Upton, who was training at Aldershot prior to departing for the front.—The service was also taken part in by ser Witlett and Mr W Bessrick.

A Former Schoolmaster's Sad End.

. _...._. _ Mr Brighou,e conducted an inquest in the Coroner's Court at noon on Tueslay,with reference to the death of William Eaton Pemberton,who was found dead in his bedroom by the police on Good Friday morning.—Thomas Stringer was the first witness called. He said he was a baker residing at 137 Mersev-str,et and the deceased was his uncle, and was 57 years of age. Deceased was a school- master by profession but for sime years he had ob- tained occasional empioyment as a clerk. He lived alone at 13, Hersey-street. On Good Fiiday morn- ing, witness was informed by P.S. Barnett that the deceased had not been seen for several days. Witness went to the deceased's house in company with the police officer and on forcing open the bedroom door, the deceased was found lying on the fl ‘or and life was extinct. Wit- ness last saw him alive on Tuesday, and he then ap- peared in eood health.—P.S. Barnett deposed that on Good Friday be was told by a neighbour of the deceased teat he had not been seen for the past few days, On forcing an entrance he found the deceased lying dead on the fioor. —Detective Bar- low, coroner's officer, stated that he had made in quiries and found that the last time the deceased was seen alive was at seven o'clock on Tuesday evening when he left the office of Mr Harrison. registrar. He then appeared in his usual state of health.—Dr Reginaid :smith, police surgeon for the borough, deposed that he had made a post mortem examination of the tody, lie found a large clot of blood on the left, side of the brain, due to cerebral hcemorrhage. There were no external marks of violence on the body with the exception of bruises on the arrn,probably sustained when befell. Unconsciousness would rapidly take place and death would occur probahly in an hour or two. Death was due to apoplexy.—A verdict in accoraance with the medical evidence was returned

A WORLD OF BOOKS.

Milions of books upon miles of Shelves. What a sentence ! Conceive. if you can, waste it implies. Who shall measure the infinite thought and labour expended by all Botta and conditions of men, ia every age and clime, upon their production ? If the enthroned fanatic who used the most magnificmt library of ancient times to heat the b;ths of Alexandria—alleging that if its con- tants were in the Kran, it was unnei,essary ; and if not, than it was mischievous—could visit the British Museum, he would find literary fuel suffi- cient for a cycle of Arctic winters. In the Museum tibia's, are bock: in every language that has a written character, practically every book in the Eng'ish tongue, and thousands of unique volumes, both manu- script and print, many of them of very great valae. A man who read for ten hours a day for a hundred years without a break, wou!ii at the end of his task, fad be had gone a very little way towards the exhaustion of John Bull's national library. In reading in old-world writings of the healing art as mactisod by ancient peoples, one is struck not so mach Lj the absurdity of their proposed remedies, horribly repugnant to modern ideas as 91 many of them are, as by the shrewdness of observation displayed in the Gee- cripti:on of symptoms. There eta be no reasonable doubt that for diors suds of years man has suffered more or less severely fr am just those very diseases which most affect him to-day. There is positive evidence th% this is So with iespect to indigestion and biliousness. The treatment alone has changed, and how vastly so Mrs. N. Valence, of St. John's Cottage, Arnaud-street, Buckland, Portsmouth, ci,n tell. Writing on January 3frOottnn.pali9ao2; this lady says :—" Following influenza, I had an attack of indigestion. I was hardly ever free but it was always worse alter eating. At iast I became afraid to eat, and grew dreadfully week in cons- quence. Constipstion,and its usual result,head- t.cbe, also troubled me. Altogether I was in a bad way, and my doctoring came to nought until I resorted to Mother Seig,el'e Curative Syrup. Re happy elects were immediate. Mr appetite improvq.l and I could eat without fear. 137 the time I had finished oae bottle of the halr.-a-crown size. I was aria to say with truth that Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup bad completely restored me to health. Since then it has become quite a family medicine with us. If the children feel at all sick or bilious, I give to, in a dose or two, and always with beneficial results.'

WARRINGTON FREE CHURCi-I COUNCIL. Annual Meeting. The Education Question. There was a good attendanee at the annual meet- ing of the Warrington Free Church Council, which was held in Bold-street Wesleyan schoolroom on Thursday last week. Sir C E Parker pre•?lded and was supported on the platform by the Revs E Lloyd Jones (formerlre of Warrington), C H Hocken, E T Reed, CI Finch, J F Matthews, and J S Hughes, THE SECRETARY'S REPO/tr. TFIE RING'S ATTiT(JDE TO WARDS HORSE RACING AND THE LIQUOR TRAFFIC. The SECRET kitY (Rev C II Hocken) presented the annual report, which read as follows : "[he greatest eat nt lest year was the organising of the services of the simultanems mission, begun February 16:h, and continued for ten days (February 26th). All the Free Chorches in the town j Aural very heartily in the mission, and the Council was well served by its missioner the R2ll J Gould. We perhaps made a inistake in not takinn nee Parr Efsll for these servicss instead of holding them in the Bold-street Chapel, and there was not sues a widespread and general spiritual awakening as some of us hoped. But the interest of the mis-,ion increased as it went on, there were large gatherinae and splendid services bringing times of refreshing and ingathering. What the final result will be we leave with God. 'rho collections and subscriptions fully tnt all the expenses of the mission, and these were kindly audited and found correct by Mr Councillor Ar bur Bonnet, leaving us with a balance in, band of is 31. In March of last year the Executive Committee made an earnest and respectful protest to the Mayor and Corporation against the decision arrived at to rebuild the Feathers Hotel, also a protest to the 51 avor and Parr Hall Committee against the evasion of the law with respect to Sunday observance by the letting of the Parr Hall for Sunday conceets. A standing sub-committee was appointed to deal with temperanc • matters both of local and national interest. Great interest was taken in the Children's Liquor Bill, and a resolutior, expressing regret and strong condemnation was seat to Mr Robert Pierpoint on account of his action ia voting aeainet the second reading of this Bill. The Executive Committee is anxious that this meeting should emphasiz s and do its utmost to insorese on all Nonconformist parent the necessity of their taking full advantage of the C insolence Clause for the protection of their children who have te attend Anglican day schools. At the last executive meeting of the year a sub-committee was appointed to make inquiries and report upon the existence of Sunday trading in the town and neighbourhood. Oa the re- ceipt of the report a temperate but vigotous circular was prepared and distributed calling the attention of ministers and leaders in churcees and schools and members of congrega ions generally to the matter, and . uiging them to no everything in their power to curtail this growing evil. At the last meeting of the Execu- tive Committee we were instructed as secretaries of the Warrington and D s riot Free Church Council to ex- plass on behalf of the committee one very loyal but heartfelt regret that his M ijesty Ed ward VII., as Monarch of these realms, should by any public action of his encourage gaantoing on the turf, Sunday desecration, or the liquor traffic,atid they deplore especially the evil influence on the nation of his Majesty's public pAronage of the drink traffic by hie recent visit to the great brewery at Burton. The finano-s of the Council are in a satisfactory p isition All tie affiliated church's have paid their annual subscription wave three and we close the year with a balance in hand of 5s 3id. In conclusion I cannot truthfully say that ' the members of the Free Churches in Warrington take an adequate interest in the work of the Free Church Council. They don' seem to realize what a miehty iostrument for good they have ready for service in the organization. of this Council. But the new year has opened well. We welcome very heartily to the chair of the Council our new Preddent, Mr 0 E Parker. We hope under his vigorous leadership we have entered on a new era and OD's more fruitful of ble!i!:- ing to Ur) free churches in this tome of Warrington." (Applause.) The CSAIRMAN said the Council was under a deep . obligation to Mr Hocken for the good work he had done as secretary during the time he had been in Warrington. (Hear; hear.) EL3 thought they would gather from the report what an influence for good the united power of t:se Ffe3 Churches of the town might be made if they had that combination which they hoped in future to attain. They had been fortunate that night in securing the presence of their old friend the Rev E Lloyd Jones. (Hear, hear.) While there were important matters which as a Council they would like to bring before the meeting, especially the great and important subject of education in which he thought Free Churches would look to the Co rued for seine guide and leading, it would be improper on their part if they put these matters in the forefront arid denied the audience the Privilege which they had come to enjoy of bearing Mr Jones very fully. (Applsui4e.) The repert was adopted. " CROMWELL AND PURITAN IDEALS." THE REV. E. LLOYD JONES'S INTERESTING LECTURE. The Rev E LLOYD JONES, who had a most cordial recep- tion, then delivered an interesting lecture on " Crom- well and Puritan Ideals." At the outset he said he knew the pulse ot Wavrington. He was in the town in the dark ages—(laughter)—and did his best to herald the ; dawn of better daye. He feared that the seed he had sown had not brought forth fruit a hundred fold,but he gatlrr cd from the report that there were some who still were the salt of the earth in Warrington. (Laughter.) Mere was hardly any great town in England and Wales that he had not visited again and again and he could say—and it would do good if he said it—that he knew of no town, where, taken upon the whole, Nonconform- ity was less heroic and Liberalism less vigorous than in Warrington. Proceeding to the subject of his lecture the rev gentleman said that howevs r great his admires tion of Cromwell was—and he could not measure that— his admiration of the Puritan principles which Cromwell so nobly represented was really greater than his admira- tion of the man. His purpose that night was to inter- pret Cromwell and Puritan ideals, and not to follow him into the mere details of his life. About a dozen yaarango, when Lord Rosebery, who was then Premier—Eugiond had now discovered that a man without backbone would not do, and if he had backbone it would greatly improve him—he was reminded that keeping race horses was not a supremo occupation for a Prime Minister, he rounded on the Nonconformists and said "How inconsistent you are. You condemn me for keeping race horses and your great prodigy Oliver Cromwell kept race horses and he was a racing man. Why don't you denounce kina or give him a fair share of denunciation ? " It was perfectly true that Cromwel loved a good horse. (Hear, hear.) So did he (the speaker but what Lord Itosebeiy did not know or cer- tainly did not state, was that all Cromwell's betting transactions were previous to his conversion and that after his conversion be returned every penny he had ever made by betting. Continuing the speaker, said that Cromwell was the great- est man this country had ever produced—the greatest lump of:manhood—and possessed the greatest amount of natural capacity for overcoming difficulties and doing the wisest thing under the circumstances. No man ever undertook to do any great task with so many opposing farces as Cromwell had to contend with. The strongest sentiments in human nature we against him. Take first oif all the eentiment of loyalty. Toalay, in the maj )rns' of instances, the sin of all sins was to criticise the public conduct of a monarch. They had travelled far and fallen very low from their Puritan ancesters who thought that the first duty of Nonconfor- mists was always to qualify their loyalty by enlighten- ment of the person,—to do all they coind to keep the I character of the monarch' clean and if it were not clean 1 to tell him it was not. There was only one paper in I England so far as he knew that even dared to give King

Edward the Seventh good advice. "The Specta- tor" dared to do it, arid "The Spect ttor " was not a puritan. They saw how tremendously strong the sentiment of loyalty was and Cromwell had to go against it. He had also to go against the sentiment of aristocracy, and of the !legal profession, and against all the privileged classes. There was hardly a great moving sentiment that was strong in human nature that Cromwell dia not attack in the name of one force —the force of an enlightened conscience. That was the test of a great man. He contended that the best way to understand Cromwell was to take Cromwell's estimate of himself. They must re- membm that Cromwell said "By birth I was a gentle- man I was born into :circumstances neither exceed- ing lowly nor exceeding high, but by birth I was a gentleman." They must not commence a study of Cromwell with the understanding that he was a vulgar agitat w, a fire brand, one of those miserable crea- tures who through any door, or window, or chimney would get into good 'society Cromwell did net do that. He was born into the very best society of this country and from his tehildhoodl all doors were practically open to him. Until about twenty five years ago the Conservatives and Churchmen did not admit the possibility that Cromwell could be a gentle- man but they had now changed their minds or at least they had been quiet about it for the last twenty five years. With regard to Puritan ideals, he thought that however muc'a they might disapprove of the methods which the Puritans used, however they might criticise their bungling methods, they must admit that what they tried to do was but the Course adopted by ninety nine En diehmen oat of a hundred to-day. Eng- land had since 1688 practically been governed on Crom- wellian principles and her late Gracious Majesty for sixty yeara willingly and lovingly did every- thing that Cron:revel ever sought or tried to do. , The Puritaiis'ideels were both political and religioue,hut more religious than political. If King Charles had had the sense to grant the people religious liberty they would have tolerated p Atticsl tyranny for a very long period. .At the bottom it was not a cry for Parliament or for a vote. It was not a cry for representation, but it was a cry for liberty to go to the Baptist Chapel. In this they saw the farsightedness of the Puritans, for after all the root of liberty was not in politics but in religion. They might make a country politically free and yet it might be religiously enslaved, but if they made a country religioesly free, he defied them to make that country politically enslaved. Man was only really free when he was free to worship and to believe according to the laws of his own thought. As regarded the Puritans, the matter politically was that Cuarles the first laboured under the delusion that all the brains of England were under his hat. He believed that no man in England was capable of forming a political opinion except himself. He died with the declaration that he woul I never grant that the people of the country had any right to have a voice in the government of the country. He believed in that mother of superstition, the divine right cf Kings. Tee human race had never beli ved ancohing as absurd as that. It had never built a super-structure upoe any foundation es iecoecavable and rotten as that. He (the speaker) declared to them that that was the most baseless belief that the human mind had ever asseeted to. Relieiously, the an:evictions of Puritans were that orey good men should make laws, that Christian religi- ous character was the only guarantee for the rectitude of a King or commoner. They craved for religioue liberty, and however limited the liberty that Cromwell gave them was, it was the first attempt to give any. He (the speaker) fancied that the majority of N incoeformiete to-day were not as advanced as Cromwell was. He gave liberty to the Presbyterians, who were his deadly foes, to the Baptists, and to the Independents, and he was willing lender favourable conditions to give liberty even th the Episcopalians. It was in his mind to give the same liberty to the Roman Catholics. Wnat was there in Epic- copelianism that made it so hateful to Puritans and what was there in Puritanism that made it se hateful to Episcopalians ? The t heory of Episcopalianism was that God's -method of conveying truth to the human mind was through material symbols, the symbols of bread, water and wine ; secondly through rt ater;al symbols that were the property of a corporation called the church ; thirdly through a body of experts who were at the nead of the Church, called the clergy. In the theory of Episcopalianism there was no contact between the mind of God and the mind of the individual. The Puritan began at the other end and said God's method of conveying truth to the human mind was not necessarily through symbols at all ; but even if it was symbols through twenty thousand or a million symbols. There was nothing from the singing of a bird to the roar of thunder which under certain conditions could not be a vehicle for conveying the divine. But that was not God's fundamental method of conveying truth. It was God Himself coining into contact with the naked mind of man, without symbols, without organizations, without experts. The Puritan revolution was not brought e bout by a let of extremists outside the Church, but by the most godly and Conser- vative men within the Church—for Croe.well was a Churchman, always a Cnurchman, and died a Church- man. In the modern sense Cromwell was not a Dis- senter, beceuse he did not believe in Disostablishment arid Disendowment. Cromwell and the Paritans, how- ever,contended that each Church should have the oppor- unity of selecting its own minister. If the majority in the parish were Baptists there must be a Baptist minister, if the majority were Congregationatist, a Con- gregational minister and so on. Cromwell lead been charged with desecrating public building,. People asked why it was that Cromwell put his horses in a cathedral. tie (the speaker) did not know, but he could imagine him doing it and would do it himself in a minute. He had not beard a single loyal subject con- demn our soldiers for doing it in the Transvaal and to the Boer his church was as holy as the English Cathedral. To him (the speaker) a coalpit was as holy, if a man was on his knees, as a pulpit and the most ignorant collier in the bowels of the earth was just as much a priest of God as the Archbishop of Canterbury. The speaker centended that the position given to the Bible by the Puritan was not tee same as that given to it by the Churchman A High Churchman imphatically said the Bible was not the immediate basis of truth ; ie was the Bible as interpreted by the Church. The Puritan said the Bible was interpreted by itself and by the living experience of man. Proceeding,the lecturer said there was not a single great reform in Church or state since the Puritan revol- ution, the thought of which had not come from the brains of the Puritaae. There was not a single act for the extension of literty, either political or religious, which had not been stolen from the brains of these men. There were a great many Nonconformists who shed many tears for the divisions of Christendom. If they asked him how many denominations there ought to be his reply was "As many as there are honest convictions in the human mind." Competition was salvation all mind. When they went where there were no divisions they were next door to hell. People asked him "Di you want Cornwell back ?" Our late Queen did everything that Cr unwell ever besought Charles to do, but if anY of her descendants played the trick of Charles and said the brains of the country were under their hat and that the people of England stood for nothing and the King for everything, that would change matters and if there was a Jones living he helped he would say "Let Croinevell come by the next train." (Applause.)

an illogical system. As result of this, we have fully half of the children of the country in so-called " Voluntary Schools." Of these " Voluntary Schools " six sevenths of the expenditure is borne by the taxes of toe country, and only one seventh comes from voluntary subscriptions ; so much for the present position. Now there was introduced into the House of Commons on Monday night by Mr Balfour, a Bill to deal with the question. I have carefully considered this Bill and am ready to acknowledge that I think it is an hon- est attempt to deal with a very difficult problem— (hear, hear)—that in some respects it would be an improvement on the present state of affairs. But consider the proposals, as far as we can judge o them in skeleton form, open to very serious objec tions: First, the Educational authority in War rington, if Warrington adopts this Local Option Bill, will be the Town Council along with a number of outsiders they may co opt- Now I have for the gentlemen who constitute our local Council a sincere respect, I honour them for the sacrifice they make of time and energy for the good of the town, but I question it they are the best men to effectively take in hand the management of the education of children. These men were returned to manage water, gas, and tramways, and to deal as economi- cally *as possible with the rates. They have no special aptitude for, or knowledge of, educational matters. It may be there are some educationalists in the Council, but that is the result of accident not design. My fear is that there will ever be pro- minent in elections, matters affecting the material interesiz of the liorough,and that education will be lost sight of and brushed out of the way. A further objectiun is the evil power which the Bill gives of tilling the country with petty denomi- national schools,and throwing the expenses of such chools on the rates. (Hear, hear.; Becauae it is roposed to enact that if there are a sufficient num- er of children—thirty I believe is the minimum mi t—whose parents do not care for the religious ducation given in the existing voluntary schools, hat they have power with the permission of the Educational Authority to erect another scheol and throwiupon the rates the Cost of its maintenance Thus we have the old evil of dividing up the chil- dren into religious groups, and herding them inco different theological pens. I look upon the exist- ing voluntary schools as a necessary evil, and that power Should be given to increase and perpetu- ate this evil is radically had. (Hear, hear). There is a great deal ef 'humbug about this Voluntary School question. It is true there are a few earnest religionists who contribute cheerfully to Voluntary Schools, but I firmly believe 90 per cent, of those who give their preference to voluntary schools do so not because they are coecorned about the form of religio instauction taaght, but because by it they escape their stare of payment for education altogether. (Hear, hear.)LAnd so it is that the voluntary system owes jr9 apparent strength, not to religious zeal, but from a desire of many who seek to avoid their fair share of the burden and responsibility of this national duty. I have been asked to refer at this meeting to what is known as the " Conseience Clause." It is not known dohether in this new Bill in view of the proposition for establish- ing new denominational schools, this " Con- science Clause " will not be swept away, bnt 83 it at present stands every parent whose convictione do not canform to the religious teaching gived in Voluntary &hoots by sectarian teachers hse the legal right to withdraw his child during the hour devoted to religious instruction. My Council desires this to be generally known and understood. But I h&Ve some hesitancy in urging the Nonconformist parents id Warrington ro put this clause into operation. I trust there is little necessity for it in Warrington. I should have still more hesitancy if I were addrressing a gattc- ing ot country or village paten's, because I know that in country districts it would otten result in social isola- tion and risk to employment and pecuniary loss which no parent ought to be called upon to bear. And further, and perhaps tnis is the strongest point I consider that a parent ought to hesitate long and anxiously. before even for censcience sake he introducea into the eehol and into his own child's school life, an element of division and separation from ite fellows, apon points which the poor innocent child can- not understand, but which may bring to the isolated child who is forced to take a position of disaent ,a bitter school life. I say that a syatem purporting to be a National system (and indeed it is a National system 50 far as payment goes):whicla places potent.' and aluldreals in such a false posit:on, cannot and ought not to last (Applause.) It is the illogical system that is at faul and n) " conscience clause " can ever put it right. We therefore invite the Free Churchmen and women ol Warrington to consider this quastion, and if they calve to the 88109 opinion as I have done, to lie/ together and not to rest satisfied until Oa authority controlling education in this tow's shall be directly elected by and be sokill7 responsible to the ratepayers. Our aim 89 Free Church- men should te '0 contend for what one of the spaaker in the H use of Commons has called the masculine element of public control, popular irigin, popular interest, and popular tnansg,ement. And I trust that the new Education Bill is to pass into Law that euoil alterations will be made in it as will render eifectiVe popular control, and through that popular coetral popular interest will be developed as it has never t een before. I trust that in England we will yet see a reallat national system directli controlled by the people, a' system which will make each citizm feel that he is moral co-ra irtner in the civic and national work ci! the schools. A feeling cf individual responsibiaty and interest cannot be developed under the present systeor I fear that in this respect, the proposed new system is no improvement. (Applause.) Cod' tinning he said be had very great pleasure le moving the following resolution :—" That this meeting of Warrington Free Churchmen is of opinion that tbe only satisiactocy soiution of the educational question je the creation of one local authority which shall Da directly elected by and responsible to the ratepayer' cor the manseement of all educational institutionsr re ceiving aid frem the rates."

Runcorn Urban Council Election.

Sir,—ln your issues of the 21st and 27th, you make reference to a report that the Co-operators intend ti run a candidate annually until they secure a majority on the Ceuncil. _ . . .

lam desired by my Committee to say that such an idea has never been entertained, and that the report is without the slightest particle of truth. Such a report is very misleading, and calcubhted to ause us a great amount of annoyance,and we shall feel obliged if you will kindly insert our most emphatic con- tradiction in your next issue.--Yours faithfully, J. The Coronation.

Sir,—ln former years I and others have subscribed to the usual Walking Day funds to the various &tools. I would now like to ask the question—will the schools expect the usual subscriptions in addition tm what will be allowed them from the general fund now being raised ? If that is so then the general fund I am afraid will suffer, because people cannot afford to give to both. Let it be clearly understood asla_whether the vaTious schools are going to forego collecting their usual subBcriptionB for this year and rely on the general fund or do they expect both. This is a question which ought to be settled at once,becanse then the subscribers can decide which fund to subscribe to.—Yours truly, S. D.

Warrington County Petty Sessions.

WEDNESDAY—Before Mr John White (in the chair), Colonel Ridgway and Mr S If Armitage. THEFT Br A WARRINGTON CARTER.—RaIph Normatt,Rose and Crown-street, Warrington, was charged with stealing a bag of coal, of the value of elevenpence. the property of the Wigan Coal and Iron Company, Limited, at Poulton, on the 6th of March.—He pleaded not guilty.—Mr Allen, of Messrs Peace and Ellis, Wigan, prosecuted on behalf of the com- pany. Be said the case arose out of one heard a fortnight sons which defendant was charged with selling a bag of .coal without having scales on the lurry. It seemed that on the 6th of March he was sent out with a lorry of coal for a customer. It was to be delivered as a whole and it wee not neceseary for him to carry scales. The inspector of weights and measures for the district saw him sell a bag to a woman at Poulton, and defendant admitted to , him that belhad.no scales. When before the Court defendant told the magistrates that he bad had a bag of coal given to him to sell by another man. The bag, however, was similar t 4 those which defendant had on the cart to deliver for the company, and defendant did not give that explanation either to the inspector or to the foreman at the yard when subsequently seen about the matter. In fact, when he got back to the yard he asked for a shilling at the wages office, and went sway and did not come back.—Evidence having been given in support of this statement, defendant, on oath, stated that he bought the bag of coal from a man In Man- chester-road. The bag was somewhat similar to the ones he had on the cart. He did not go back to the company's yard because he was a,hamed of having been caught by the inspector.—A fine of ifs and costs was imposed. ARRESTED oN SIISPICION.—James Dailey was charged with being found in possession of a pair of stcckings; a singlet, lady's blouse and other articles of wearing apparel and being unable to give a satisfactory account of them.—P.S. Mellor, stationed at Padgate, said he met the prisoner in Manchester road, Woolston and noticed that his pockets were bulky. On being questioned prisoner said he had some bread in thep], but on witness searching the man he found the articles named In the charge. Prisoner stated that be had bought them.— Prisoner still adhered to the statement that he had bought the articles, Informing the Bench that he intended to make , neckties with the blouse. (Laughter).—He was remanded fora week, MISCELLANEOUS OFFENCES.—John McDermott was fined 5s for being in charge of a horse and not having any reins attactted to it at Rixton on March 27th.—Martin Higgin was fined a similar amount for a like offence.—Nicholas Worthington, John Murty, William Southern. Richard Williams, Nathan Darbyebire and Edward Rapsou were summoned for gambling in Fairciough-street. Burtonwoud on March 16th.—P.O. Gerrie having given evidence the magistrates tined Murty and Derbyshire (who made their appearance) is and costs, Rapson, Worthington and Southern (who had been before the Court once previously) 2s 6d and costs, and Williams (who made his sixth appearance)ss and costs; James Dowd and John Walsh were fined Zs 6d and costs for a similar offence ; Thomas Garry, Nathan.Darbyshire,William Worthington and Ernest Walker were also summoned for gambling at Burtonwood. Gravy who bad not been summoned before was fined Is and costs. Walker and Worthington were fined 2s 6d and costs, and Derbyshire 5s and costs—Samuel Rumble was summoned or obstructing the highway at Rixton on March 3rd by allowing a traction engine and two waggons to stand there from 6.30 to 12 p.m.—A fine of 10s and costs was inflicted.

Success of the Apollo Male Voice Choir. At the Bisteddlod held at Parr, St Helens, on Easter Monday, the Warrington Apollo Male Voico Choir competed and cariied off the first prize of twe guineas for their excellent rendering of " Comrades tong of lope," for which they were warmly ap- plauded. The choir, which comprises 20 members, was under the conductorship of Mr T Brough.—The judge (MrCadwyn Owen, of Liverpool), thus re- ferred to the successful choir:—" This choir was running a great risk in not having an accompanist, as there is always a tendency for a choir to go out of tune, but this choir kept the pitch, and there its great credit due to them. It had a perfect balance, beautiful blend of voices and pure tone. With regard to the piece the opening passages were most beautiful. The allegro movement was splendid,and from here to the finish it was a grand performance, and I haverfot the slightest hesitation in awarding l'l*o 2 the prize."—lt should be stated that at the mune competition, Mr Greenough, of Warrington, obtained the first prize for his singing of a tenor solo " Fairest maiden." The judge remarked that Mr Greenough's Was a splendid interpretation of the solo. Mr P Smith, a member of the Apollo Choir, obtained the first prize in the bass solo competition entitled " Mau of Sorrows." The judge said the selection was excellently given and Mr Smith possessed a splendid voice. Mr Nathan Cooke, alPo a member of the choir, obtained a prize in an im- promptu-speech competition. LATEST NEWS. Mine Explosion Explosion Near Wigan.

Seven Men Killed.

On Wednesday night a very serious explosion took place at a sinking pit owned by the Gars wood Hall Colliery Company, and situate on the boundaries of Ashton-in-Makerfield and Golborne, near Wigan.

The pit, which is being sunk to the Arley mine at a depth of 450 yards, is 260 yards deep at present. Ten men, eight sinkers and two bricksetters, were at work at the bottom of the shaft, and at five minutes past eight there appears to have been a sudden outburst of gas from a feeder. This gas made its way :up the shaft and becoming ignited evidently at or near the surface led to a terrific explosion. The headgear, 40 feet in height, was literally blown down. A large area of staging, with the cabins thereon, was wrecked, one huge baulk of timber being blown 20 yards, whilst a smaller piece was hurled across a field to a distance of 100 yards. Windows and frames as well were blown out of the engine bouses,and the dynamo attendant was struck en the nose with a frame. THE SURVIVORS. The names of the survivors are : Robert Davis, 309, Potter's-row, Bolten-road, Ashton- in-Makerfield.

Patrick Stanton, Ten Houses, Bolton-road, Ashton-in- Makerfield.

Thomas Walden, 13ryn Gates, Bamfurlong. John Wall, 55, Bamford-street, Parr, St Helens. THE KILLED.

The names of the deceased are : Michael Tughey.l3s, Warrington-Icm], Abram. David Jones, 56, Heath-street, Golborne. John Griffiths, 19,Lily-street, Stubstiawcross, Asht on in-Mak erfield.

Carhuman, a Polish Jew. 5, Reservoir•street, Ashton- in-Makerfield, William Hurst, Ashton-in-Ilakerfield.

Thomas Gilbert, Astiton-in-Makerfield. These, with the man Taylor, of Warrington-road Abram, the browman, who was hurled off the stage and killed, make seven who have lost their lives. Nearly all are married men. As the dawn broke the scene around revealed the terrible force of the explosion. Huge lumps of timber, 14 inches square, of which the headgear was made, lie scattered over the adjoining field, partially splintered into matchwood, whilst the ironwork is bent and broken in amazing fashion.

THE WAR.

LATEST TELEGRAMS.

Sharp Fighting.

Queen's Bays Severely Engaged.

Four fficers Wounded.

The following despatch was issued from the War Office on Wednesday night From Lord Kitchener to the Secretary of State for War. _ _

Pretoria, 2nd April. 11.50 a.m. (9 a.m., 2nd April).— Colonel Lawley reports sharp fighting from Boschmsn's Kop.

On the evening of the 31st March he detached the 2nd Dragoon Guards to work ten miles to the eastward, intending to co-operate in the same direction on the follwing morning.

At dawn yesterday the 2nd Dragoon Guards succeeded in surprising a Boer laager, but the enemy, being strongly re-inbreed, held their ground, and after a severe engagement the 2nd Dragoon Guards had to fight a rearguard action to rejoin. Their main tody, which was advancing towards the firing, came iilto action and drove the enemy to the north-east.

Lairley reports Captains Herron and W Lieutenant Htll,2nd Dragoonpuards,and Major Vaughan 71h Hussars wounded.

Details of casualties will be reported separately.

The enemy's loss is reported severe. Six prisoners have been brought in.

Presentation to a Walton Volunteer. A smoking concert was beld at the Stag Hotel,Walton the other night, when Private John Beswick, who hss volunteered for active service in South Africa, was pre- sented with a marble timepiece, the gift of his fellow workmen ; a cigarette holder and half a pound of cigar- ettes, the gift of Mr Underhill, the landlord. Mr Massey, one of the first contingent of volunteers who went from Warrington to South Africa, took the chair, and songs were ably rendered by Messrs Gleave, Pemberton,Davies and others.

Manx Volunteers Embark for Warrington.

A detachment of nine men of the Douglas Volunteer Corps (attached Co the Liverpool Regiment) left the Isle of Man on Tuesday morning for Warrington, where they will in a few days be drafted off on active service in South Africa. Daisy's the second detachment that has left the Isle of Man for the South Afrim war, and as on the previous occasion the departure of the men by the morning steamer was witnessed by largo crowds, who heartily cheered the men. The whole corps and band accompanied the men to the pier, where the scene was very enthusiastic. On Monday night the men going oat were entertained to dinner at the Grand Hotel by the officers of the corps. Letters were read from the Governor, Lord Henniker, regretting not being present. His Excellency spnt cardigan jackets for each man, who also received a pipe and Testament from the chaplain and woollen clothing from the officers. Captain Mac- kenzie was in the chair, and hearty good wishes were extended to the young fellows. The men entered Orford Barracks the same evening, and are now awaiting orders for their departure for the front. . -

A detachment, consisting of one sergeant, one bugler, and 24 rank and file, comprising representatives of the let, 2nd, 41%, 6th, and sth (Liverpool Scottish) Volun- teer Battalions of the gin's (Liverpool Regiment) arrived at the depot, Warrington, on Tuesday. The detachment was under the command of Lieutenant J Fisber, 4th Liverpool (V). Ansoog those present at the departure from the Central Station, Liverpool, were Colonel De Silva and Lieutenant Rosenheim. The detachment were joined at Warrington depot by 15 men of the 3rd Liverpool (V) from Southport.

SlseSTgons Fns.—On Sunday, at Frodsbam, the boot shop and clog:gees shop belonging to Mr Hindley, and the tinsmith's shop belonging to Mr Rothwell were com- pletely dastroyed by fire. Personal.

At Birkenhead Easter Eisteddfod on Monday, Miss Shaw Green, Warrington. was awarded the prize of 10s 6d in the handworked table centre competition.

STREET'S NEWSPAPER DIRECTORY, 1902, (30, Cornhill, London), is a handsome portly volume of some four hundred pages, well-bound in coloured cloth, with gilt lettering and illustration of " Lincoln's Inn, Old Gate- way, 1513" ; is idmirably printed on toned paper,with red borderinv,a. Its appearance, indeed, is such as too constitute it one of the most attractive features of an ordinary Newspaper office or general advertiser's library, while to the latter it will prove a constant and faithful ' vade mecum. There is presented with it one large man of England and Wales, with smaller sectional maps of Scotland and Ireland, all tastefully and artistically executed in light and agreeable colours. Names of the towns having one newspaper are printed in blue, those boasting two in red,and others glorying in three or more in green. The work has been carefully and successfully arranged with • view to the readiest means of reference, and contains complete lists of London newspapers, daily and weekly, and of magazines published monthly and quarterly. Class and trade papers have much space assigned to them, as have also the various religious papers and periodicals. There are two pages devoted to lists of provincial towns having more than 100,000 population,tolhose'with more than fifty but fewer than 100,000, and ts others with more than twenty but fewer than fifty thousand. The newspaper advertisements, with a copious index, appear in the latter part of the work.

The annual vestry meeting was held in Wargrave Church on Tuesday evening, but there was only a poor attendance of the parishioners. The Rev J Carson pre- sided.
