WARRINGTON BUTCHERS' ASSOCIATION.

First Annual Dinner.

At the Fatten Arms Hotel, on Thursday evening, the members of the Warriugton Butchers' Association, cele- brated their first annual dinner. There was an excellent attendance, including delegates from several centres. Mr Albert Alynelcyne presided, and amongst those present were :—Mr C Pearson, of Sheffield, (president of the National Federation of Butchers), Mr Hill (Sheffield Association), Mr F Greer (president of the Liverpool District Association), Mr J Nish (Liverpool), Mr John Chadwick and Mr Fletcher (St Helens), Alder- , man Sutton, Couiacillcir James Evans, Councillor John Sampson, Messrs Joseph Knowles, John Mainwaring, B Shearer, with the following officials of the Warrington Association :—Mr G Bowcock (vice-president), Mr J Bowcock and Mr El Singleton (ex-presidents), Mr J Uns- worth (secretary), Mr F Lashtord (treasurer). Messrs F Connolly, S Cross, R C Shaw, R T Bridge, T Everall and T Rigby (committee). At the conclusion of an excellent repast, which had been excellently served under the superinteadeace of Miss Kiernan, a toast list was submitted. The loyal toasts were drunk with musical honours.

Mr SINGLETON then proposed the toast of the National Federation and kindred Associations,and coupled with it the name of Mr C Pearson, of Sheffield, president of the National Federation. He remarked that the local association considered itself highly honoured by being favoured with the presence ot Mr Pear+on at the first annual dinner. He hoped Me Pearson would be able to influence the members of the Association, and especially the young members to do the best they could for the organization. He had never known a time when there was more need for them to bind themselves together than the present. (Hear, hear). No doubt many of them had read in the papers a great deal about what was called the American Meat Trust and anyone going to the markets to purchase meat could see that the Americans had got the English butchers in a corner. The only way the butchers could defend themselves against this monopoly was through the influence of the National Federation of butchers. (Hear, hear.) They could not do it as ,individual associations but it could be done through the medium he hid stated. He believed the Federation would have to cake the matter up with a very strong hand. It was possible, he thought, for them to induce the Government to enable Canadian cattle to come; into this country free. It was a sarious matter for the butchers of Warrington and farmers who are feeders el cattle in the neighbourhood and the Government's attention ought to be called to the matter. (Hear, hear.) Mr Peensoer, in responding, congratulated the War- rington Association that they had 45 members out of 60 , butchers in the town. It was a very big percentage, and he hoped the time was not far distant when every member of the trade would be connected with the fine- elation. The Nationel Federation existed more particularly to assist associations. He was glad to ' hear that the local authority was acting in sympathy with the butchers, (Hear, hear). It was not so in every town, In Sheffield they bad a gleat difficulty on the subject of tuberculosie. He did not think any town suffered from that disease more than Sheffield. The evidence of butchers before the Royal Commis- Mon which eat on the subject showed that the butchers were alive to their own interest and through the influence of the Federation steps had been Won by the Government to try and avoid in some measure the great seizures of tuberculosis cattle. The Federation maintained that if confiscation was to take place of what appeared healthy cattle when they were bought, the butchers were entitled to com- pensation for them. (Hear, hear.) He was now refer- ring to animals that zoat over £lO. 'They did not ask i for compensatien for animals that cost less than that sum. He well remembered the sensation that was caused at the meeting in Leocton when Dr Koch announced that consumption was not communicable from animals Co man. Butchers had never imagined it was so, but when Professor Koch made that statement it caused such a sensation as he had never known in his life. Medical scientists had to receive sentiments of that sort with a certain amount of reserve. He (the speaker) honoured the medical profession because they had an object in view and that was tha protection of the health of the public. The butchers tried to act with them and not against them. He could state many cases where injury had been done to butchers by the seizure of :cattle. lie had known a butcher who had bought a beast which represented the whole of his capital, and then found it condemned on the ground that it was suffer ,n 1 from tuberculosis. They were all hoping that the statements made by Pro- fessor Koch would prove to be true. This injustice of confiscation—this legisliaed robbery—was one of those things butchers did not intend to submit to any longer than they could possibly help. He was glad Mr Singleton had raised the question of foreign cattle. At the present time they were suffering from a lack of foreign cattle. The United States were sending to this country just their surplus supplies in order to keep the price up in tbe States and to enable them to dictate the prices to be paid bete. He was at one with Mr Singleton that they must use their influence to get the embargo on Canadian stores removed. The farmers at the present time had 3 diffi- culty in buying their stores at is reasonable fieure and if the embargo were removed it would be a benefit to the farmer, and a benefit to the butcher, and anything that was a benefit to the butcher was a bene- fit to the public. The Federation. bad the matter in hand and he hoped their efforts would be satisfactory. He believed that if the Government could be induced to allow South American cattle to COM into this country, which was a great necessity, the American people would be more amenable to reason. He proceeded to refer to the importance of butchers using their influence at election times for those candi- dates who would help them and also of obtaining repre- sentation on the Borough Council. In cencluiion Mr Pearson referred to the great losses sustained by butchers in the sale of hides owing te the destructiveness of the warble fly. He complained that this loss was due to carelessness on the part of farmers who could easily prevent the injury to the hides by a little care. The loss to butchers was equsl to 5s a hide, and the loss to farmers was a great deal more. He con- gratulated the Warrington Association on its vitality, and said he was not only pleased to see Mr Shaw, who he was told was the oldest butcher in the trade, tailing an interest in the association, but also such a number of young members. (Hear, bear)

Councillor Bennett and the Late Mr. Rhodes. —Meeting of Conservatives. On Saturday an executive Committee meeting of the Lancashire and Cheshire Federation was held in the Manchester Conservative Club. Representa- tives were present from Liverpool, Wigan, Mossley, Newton Heath, Heywood, Altrincham, Warrington. Wigan (St. Andrew's), West Salford, Pendleton, Manchester, and the secretary:(Mr F W Deacon).— Councillor A Bennett, of Warrington, presided. In opening the proceedings he said all of that com- mittee he felt sure, had heard with deep regret of the death of Mr Cecil Rhodes. The Empire had lost one of its noblest sons' and it was difficult to ex- aggerate the services he had rendered.. He moved that a sincere expression of sympathy should go forth from that Federation. Other members hav- ing spoken in similar terms, it was resolved that the same be entered upon the minutes.— Mr E Bentham (secretary Wigan Operative Con. servative Association),moved : "That this executive, whilst accepting the principle of registration ot clubs contained in the proposed licensing Bill as a legitimate source of revenue, enters its emphatic protest against any right of interference, or inspec- tion by the police, with such clubs when registered. It further resents the proposed exorbitant fines and insulting pmalizing clauses of the said Bill as uncalled for and unjust." Mr Ferneley seconded, and Mr F W Deacon supported the resolution which was carried by a large majority. After consider- able discussion on the subject of education pro- posals, the following resolution was unanimously passed: —"That this meeting views with favour the endeavour of the Government to place the educa- tion of the country on a broad and national basis, approves of its general provisions, but considers the permissive clauses contained therein will largely defeat the end in view, and if acted upon completely nullify the main objects of the Bill." ACCIDENT TO MRS. CARS)N.

On Thursday morning- an accident which might have had serious results happened to .ire Canon, wife of the Rector of Newton. She was crossing the road under Wargrave bridge when she was knocked down by a greengrocer's cart.lt was feared that she was badly injured, but after being removed to the rectory and attended by Dr Valen- tine, it was found that beyond the shock and a few bruises she was not seriously hurt. Mrs Carson is now in a fair way to recovery.

EARLEST.IWN WESLEYAN MEN'S BIBLE CLASS.

The annual picnic in connection with the above class took place on Easter Monday to Colwyn Bay. The party, numbering about 50, left Earlestown in two saloons at 7 13,and reached Colwyn Bay in due course; Their headquarters were made at Moon's Restaurant, where everything possible was done for their comfort. The weather was very fine and a most enjoyable evening was.spent on the beach and in the woods, which were just beginning to put on their spring raiment. On the return journey about an hour and a half was spent in Chester, an oppor- tunity being given for a ramble round the ancient city. The arrangements were satisfactorily carried out by Mr D Pickett.

===== 0002601_19020404_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EASTER HOLIDAYS AT EARLESTOWN.

Most of the works having closed on Thursday night last until Tuesday morning, there was a large exodus of people to the various holiday resorts, the majority being those who could avail themselves of the privilege of quarter fare. Considering the early period of the year on which Easter has fallen, the weather was not at all bad, and those who remained at home were able to enjoy a walk into the country. Special services were held in most of the churches and the chapels, and at St. John's Church there was a celebration of Holy Communion after the morning service, a large number of wor- shippers being prPsent.—On Good Friday afternoon the Earlestown football ground was crowded, the occasion being a League encounter between Earles- town and Rochdale Town, in which the home team were victorious by the handsome majority of 6 goals to nil. The Earlestown Band was present, and after the match played for dancing.—On Mon- day the morning opened dull, and continued so throughout the day, but at night rain began to fall, making it most unpleasant for holiday makers.

NOTES BY THE NAY AT

E ARLESTOwN.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT]

The Easter holiday, although it has fallen so early this year, was entered into with the greatest zest so tar as Earlestown and Newton were concerned. There was a large exodus of people to various parts of the country, and there was a fairly numerous influx to tbe town. The weather on Good Friday was very fine, and the match on the Earlestown football ground was the means of attracting a good crowd, an additional attraction being the presence of 'the Earlestown Band, which after the match played for dancing. On Saturday and Monday there were no attractions in the town, and conse- quently many people made journeys to Manche,- ter and Liverpool (tor New Brighton.)

-- Unfortunately the holidays did not pass over without an 6Vent which ,does relouni to the credit of the town, or its residents. A drunken brawl occurred in a portion of the town not notable for its respectability, as a result of which one of the disturbers of the peace was stabbed. The police were quickly on the scene, and the tasile of the police court was the sequel.

The unfortunate explosion which happened the other side of Ashton, on Wednesday , night, caused much alarm in Earlestown and Newton. The noise was distinctly heard, and the shock was telt all over the district. Many thought it was an earth- quake shock, but those accustomed to mining operations soon arrived at a solution and within the space of an hour it was known at Earlestown that an explosion had occurred, but the extent of the disaster was not fully known.

===== 0002601_19020404_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
SOUTH AFRICA.

With the better tone in the market there have been some buyers of "Little Kaffirs." One of the cheapest of these is North Sheba, which at about 2s 6d to 35 for the £1 fully-paid share is a good speculation for a sharp rise. 1113 mine is well known, being in the Barberton district on what is believed to be the same line of reof as the famous Sheba property, and moreover so much development work has been dbne on the North-Sheba that to a great extent the property has been proved. The company holds some ninety claims, and enough ore has been already opened up. it is said, to keep a twenty stamp battery supplied for three years. An encourag- ing feature is that at depth the reef has widened out, extending over some 15 feet at the 300 foot level. Sam- ples taken right over the width of the reef have been certified by Johnson, Matthey and Company to give an average value of 1i or, as against this may be set the belief that an'average yield of 5 dwt, could be made to pay, therefore the shares are likely to prove an excellent pu,chase. " Money."

RUNCORN NOTES.

===== 0002601_19020404_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
[BY A CORRESPONDENT.]

Readers will observe that the Runcorn Co-opera- tive Society has sent a letter, which appears in another column, indignantly repudiating the insin- uation that they intended to run candidates until they secured a majority on the Urban Co uncil. It is, of course,quite evident that it would take years to bring such a scheme to full fruition, for the ordinary man in the street treated the rumour as the canard it wag. Thera are, however, a 'few shal- low paced individuals who actually believed that the So2iety had really formulated such an idea. And to such individuals the letter will come as a severe rebuke.

Beyond the discussion which arose on the pro- pOsed taking over by the Council of the Cottage Hospital there was but little business to be done. Mr Posnett was well advised to refer his resolution to a committee for, to say the least of it, the taking over of the hospital at the present stage would have been decidedly premature. Before the Coun- cil assume responsibility it should be clear that no unnecessary expense is to be incurred. While the town would probably subscribe yearly a handsome donation to such a worthy object, it is rather a questionable step to ask the Council to assume financial responsibility so long as there is an amount of uncertainty about the undertaking. Mr Posnett took a businesslike step in accepting the suggestion of Messrs Wiswall and Handley.

===== 0002601_19020404_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOTBALL.

Football on Good Friday was at a discount at Runcorn. The rival clubs Runcorn and Widnes always produce keen interest when honours are to be fought for, and an exceptionally large crowd visited Irwell-lane in ant cipation ot a good game. But the sightseers were woefully disappointed. Both clubs had special engagements on the Satur- day, and could not afford to play their first team men. Thus it was practically a match between the se3ond teams. The Chemicals won and won fairly. They were superior in every department, and the Linnets were lucky to get off as lightly as they did. Still it was not a proper first class match, and is by no means a criterion of the strength of the respec- tive clubs.

The cup will not this season find a resting place in Runcorn. The locals lost by a point at Castle- ford, which is hard luck indeed and the least said about the game is the best. It has been asserted that the match was lost entirely through Sam Houghton's faulty play. If this be correct it is hardly fair to censure him as severely as there is a tendency to do in certain quarters. Even a Hough- ton or a Jolley cannot always be in form and we should look at the veteran's past brilliant successes when he has pulled the game out of the fire rather than think of him harshly because, for once, he has not played Eo well as he might have done.

The Saintly Saints visited the Runcorn enclosure on Monday and went home wiser and, let us hope, better men. They were thoroughly outclassed in every department, though, at times, their forwards played a grand game. Roe,lly there seemed to be very little interest in the march from the players as well 89 the spectators point of view. The drubbing at Castle Ford has taken all the earnestness out of foot`sallers in Runcorn for a time.

The "Athletic News " has the following inter- esting paragraph respecting one of our foremost football officials .—Hr John Ellison,secretary to the Runcorn F. 0., unlike most club officials, has never donned a jersey, but he has ever been a devoted a imirer of the game. Nearly 20 years since he was an enthusiastic follower of Runcorn Club, 16 year; ago he was made assistant secretary to the late Mr E Lanzshaw, whom he succeeded in 1898. fie is an energetic, painstaking, able official, who pos- sesses the confidence of both committee and play era, and represents the club on the Northern League. Mr Ellison is also secretary to the South- West Lancashire Competition.

REMARKABLE STORY FROM WIDNES.

Alleged Extraordinary .Robbery.

0 t WedaesAsy a strange robbery is supposed to have occurred in Widnes, the victim being a woman named Thompson living in Dicksou-stvet. A man who had a confederate in the vicinity, visited the house, presum- ably under the guise of a pilot igrapher. After asking the woman if she oared to have a photograph taken, she ;cos asked if her hu,band would like his taken. Mrs Thompson told the man that be was working, that she had no lodgers, and the children were out playing, whereupsn he went inside the house, followed by the accomplice and demanded money. It is also alleged that they used violent threats and tied her to the mangle. After receiving a sum of money, supposed to be large, Caey decamped. g Superintendent Keightley and Inspector Strickland visited the house, but are reticent in giving any particu- lars before the C 489 is investigated. Tho affair has caused a sensation in the neighbour- hood where the victimised woman lives.

Our correspondent, upon making further inquiries has gleaned a few more important facto. The man,after the refusal by the woman to be photographed, asked for a drink of water. She went to get this, and, when half-way up the lobby, on turning round, saw the man follow her. He produced a revolver and threatentd her if she made a noise. The woman was at once ragged and tied to the mangle* After this process he went to the back doer sad called in his ix,ate, who was likewise arm•d. They used further threats, and one of them, taking the wick out of the lamp, placed it near her clothes, which became ignited. Both men, it is said, possessed dark lanterns. About £BO t 3 £lOO is supposed to have been stolen.

A Warrington Footballer's Sad Death. Mr Cecil Holden, coroner for Birkenhead, presi- ded on Wednesday over an inquest regarding the death of Sidney Barnes, a private in C Company, South Lancashire Regiment, stationed at Orford Barracks, Warrington. Barnes, who was twenty- three years of age, and belonged to Operishaav, Man- chester, was a member of the Association Football Club connected with the barracks. On the after- noon :of Easter Monday he played with that team against the Earlestown Thursday Football Club in a Liverpool District Thursday League match, on the ground at Fronton Park, Birkenhead, and during the game be collided with the opposing goalkeeper's knee. lie received internal injuries, and Dr Leask, who was called to the ground to attend the injured man, directed his removal in the horse ambulance to the Borough Hospital. There he died on Wed- nesday morning. Atte; taking formal evidence of identification the Coroner adjourned the inquiry to Thursday. 30,000 BLIND PHOPLII have had their sight restored by MELLOR, 168, Oxford-street Manchester. EASTERTIDE AT rtYNCOR N.

The Easter holidays hav-e-bee—n-o-b-s;Wed at Run- corn in much the same fashion as in previous years, On Good Friday morning all the places of worship held special fiery ices which were well attended. The weather being oF a very promising character,in spite of a dull morning, many journeyed out of town, cycli.ts heincr especially numerous. Raster Monday found the town all but deserted, everybody apparently taking advantage of the fine weather to journey to "fields and pastures new.' Numbers went to Overton hills and still larger numbers to Liverpool and popular summer resorts. On the whole the holidays have been utilised by everyone to their fullest extent.

COUNTY BOROUG ff OF WARRINGTON.— CORONATION OF' H.M. RING EDWARD VII. At the request of the Committee appointed to carry out the arrangements for Celebrating the Coronation of N.M. the King I beg to appeal to the Inhabitants of the Town and Neighbourhood for their assistance to (suable the Committee to carry out the followino objects :- I.A PUBLIC PROCESSION in which the TRADES, the FRIENDLY SOCIETIES and OTHER RE- PRESENTATIVE BODIES will CO-OPBRATB on the day of the Coronation. 2. A PROOBsSION OF SCHOOL CHILDREN to take place on the following day. 3. A. TREAT TO SCHOOL CHILDREN. 4. THE DECORATIoN and ILLUMINATION of the PUBLIC and other BUILDINGS in the Town. 5. A PERMANENT MEMORIAL of the Coronation. 5. COMMEMORATION MEDALS. 7. A TREAT TO THE AGED POOR. It is estimated that at least £2,000 will be required for the above objects. Subscriptions in aid of the whole or any of the above objects may be paid Into any of the Local Banks or to the Borough Treasurer at Bank House. 7. CHARLTON PARR, Mayor. Town Hall, Warrington, March 21st, 1902. LIST OF SUBSCRIP.CIONS. g a. d.
